## st-0212 — The Three Little Pigs
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
Three small children live in a country. They are a family.

They want to make their own houses. Each child wants one house.

The first child does not like work. He wants to play all day.

He makes his house with some paper. He does it in a short time.

He says, 'This is good for me.' Then he goes to play.

The second child works a bit more. But he does not want hard work.

He makes his house with small sticks. It is easy to build.

He thinks, 'This will hold against wind.'

The last child works hard. He wants a strong house.

He makes his house with big rock. It takes many days.

He feels good when it is done.

One night, a big bad man comes. He goes to the first house.

He hits the door with his hand. He says, 'Let me in!'

The child says, 'No, not by my face!'

The man gets very bad. He blows hard at the house.

The paper house falls down. The child runs away.

He runs to his friend’s stick house.

The man follows him. He hits the stick house door.

The children say, 'No, not by our faces!'

The man blows hard. The stick house falls too.

Both children run to the rock house.

The man comes to the rock house. He tries to blow it down.

The house does not move. It is too strong.

The man thinks of a plan. He says, 'I know a place with food.'

He tells the children to meet him there. They must come early the next day.

The children say yes. But they leave very early.

They get the food first. The man comes but finds no one.

He gets bad again. He goes back to the rock house.

He goes up the side of the house. He wants to come down the top.

The hard-working child hears the man. He puts water in a big box.

He puts the box over a light. The water gets hot.

The man falls into the water. He comes out with a big sound.

He runs away. He never comes back.

The three children are safe now. They live in the rock house.

They learn a thing. Hard work is good.

### A2
Once, in a green country, three little boys lived together. They were brothers.

They decided to make their own houses. Each boy wanted a good home.

The first boy did not like hard work. He wanted to finish soon.

He used small bits of soft plant matter to make his house. It was fast to build.

He said, 'This will do for me.' He sat down to rest.

The second boy was a bit different. He did not want to work too hard.

He used small bits of tree parts for his house. It was faster than big rocks.

He thought, 'This will hold well.' He felt happy.

The third boy knew strong houses take time. He wanted his house to last.

He used big rocks to build his house. It took many days to finish.

He felt very happy when it was done. His house was the strongest.

One night, a big bad dog came. He saw the first boy’s house.

He went to the door and hit it. He said, 'Little boy, let me in!'

The boy said, 'No, not by my hair!' The dog got very cross.

He sent a strong wind at the plant house. The house fell down fast.

The boy ran away. He went to his brother’s house.

That house was made of tree parts. The dog followed him.

The dog hit the tree part house door. He said, 'Let me in!'

The boys said, 'No, not by our hair!' The dog sent a strong wind again.

The tree part house fell too. Both boys ran to their brother’s house.

That house was made of big rocks. The dog came to the rock house.

He tried to send a strong wind at it. The house did not move.

The dog thought of a plan. He said, 'Boys, I know a place with good food.'

He told them to meet him there early. The boys said yes.

But they left even earlier. They got the food first.

The dog came to the field. He did not see the boys.

He got cross and went to the rock house. He went up on top.

He wanted to come down the top opening. The third boy heard him.

The boy put a big container on the fire. The water inside got very hot.

The dog fell into the hot water. He came out with a loud sound.

He ran away fast. He never came back again.

The three boys were happy. They stayed in the rock house.

They knew hard work was best. They lived together well.

### B1
Once, in a green land, there were three small animals. They were brothers.

One day, they decided to make their own houses. Each brother wanted a house that was safe and warm.

The first brother did not like hard work. He wanted to finish fast. He used dry plants to build his house. It was quick and easy. Then he played all day. He said, 'This is good for me!'

The second brother was not lazy. But he still did not want to work too much. He used sticks to build his house. It was faster than using stone. He thought, 'This will hold against most things.'

The last brother was careful and worked hard. He knew a strong house needed time. He used stone to build his house. It took many days. But he felt happy when it was done.

One evening, a big, bad dog came to the first brother’s house. He knocked on the door. He said, 'Little animal, let me come in!'

The first brother said, 'No, I will not let you in!' The dog got angry. He blew hard at the dry plant house. With one big blow, the house fell down.

The first brother ran away fast. He ran to his brother’s house made of sticks.

The dog followed him. He knocked on the stick house. The two brothers said, 'No, we will not let you in!'

The dog blew hard again. The stick house fell too.

Both brothers ran to their brother’s stone house. The dog came and tried to blow it down. But the house was too strong. No matter how hard he blew, it did not move.

The dog thought of a plan. He said, 'Little animals, I know a field with sweet food.' He told them to meet him there early the next morning.

The brothers agreed. But they left even earlier to get the food first.

The dog came to the field. He found no animals there. He got angry and went to the stone house.

He climbed onto the top of the house. He planned to come down through the smoke hole.

The careful brother heard the dog. He put a big container of water on the fire.

When the dog came down the smoke hole, he fell into the hot water. He jumped out with a big sound. He ran away and never came back.

The three brothers were safe at last. They have learned that hard work is important. From that day, they lived happily in the stone house.

They often talked about what happened. They said, 'If we work hard, we will always be safe.'

The first brother said, 'I have learned my lesson.' He promised to work harder from then on.

The second brother agreed. He said, 'I will not be slow to work again.'

The last brother smiled. He was glad his brothers now understood. He knew they would all be safe together.

The brothers lived well after that. They grew food in their garden. They had many friends in the village. Everyone liked them because they were kind.

Sometimes, they told their story to others. They wanted to help people learn the same thing. They said, 'If you work hard, good things will happen.'

The brothers stayed close. They shared everything they had. They were happy and safe in their strong house.

One day, a friend asked them about their house. The first brother said, 'We built it with stone.' He added, 'It took time, but it was worth it.'

The second brother said, 'We have not had any trouble since.' He smiled and said, 'It is the best house we could have.'

The last brother nodded. He said, 'If you build with care, you will be safe too.'

Their friend thought about this. He decided to build his own strong house. He asked the brothers to help him.

They worked together for many days. When the house was done, they all felt proud. The friend said, 'Thank you for showing me how to do it right.'

The brothers were glad to help. They knew that hard work would always bring good results. They said, 'We are happy to share what we have learned.'

From that time, more people in the village built strong houses. They saw how safe the brothers were. They wanted the same for their families.

The brothers often walked through the village. They saw the new houses and smiled. They felt good about what they had started.

One evening, they sat outside their house. They talked about the past. They remembered the day the dog came.

The first brother said, 'I will never forget that day.' He looked at his brothers and said, 'We have come a long way.'

The second brother agreed. He said, 'We have learned so much since then.'

The last brother said, 'We are stronger together.' He added, 'That is the most important thing.'

They all nodded. They knew their hard work had made their lives better. They were ready for whatever might come next.

The brothers continued to live well. They helped others and were kind. Their story became a lesson for everyone in the village.

People often said, 'If you work hard, you can have a good life.' The brothers smiled when they heard this. They knew it was true.

### B2
Once, in a green land, there lived two little pigs and their brother. They were a happy family and decided it was time to make their own homes.

Each pig wanted a home that would keep them safe and warm through the year. The first little pig was lazy and did not want to work hard at all.

He built his house very fast with dry grass so he could play all day. He laughed and said, 'This is good enough for me!'

The second little pig was a bit smarter but still did not like hard work. He built his house with sticks because it was easier than using stone.

He thought to himself, 'This will hold against most things that come.' The last little pig was wise and careful about his choices.

He knew that a strong house needed time and much effort to build well. So he built his house with stone, which took many days to complete.

When it was done, he felt very proud of what he had made. One evening, a large, hungry animal came to the first pig’s house.

He knocked on the door and called out, 'Little pig, let me come in!' The pig replied, 'No, not by the hair on my face!'

The animal got angry and blew hard at the house made of dry grass. With one big blow, the house fell down, and the pig ran away fast.

He ran to his brother’s house that was made of sticks. The animal followed and knocked on the door of the stick house.

Again, the pigs said, 'No, not by the hair on our faces!' The animal blew hard, and the stick house fell too.

Both pigs ran to their brother’s stone house as fast as they could. The animal came and tried to blow the house down, but it was too strong.

No matter how hard he blew, the house did not move at all. The animal thought of a trick and said, 'Little pigs, I know a field with sweet roots.'

He told them to meet him there early the next day. The pigs agreed but left even earlier to get the roots first.

The animal came to the field but found no pigs there. He got very angry and went to the stone house, climbing onto the top.

He planned to come down through the top opening to catch the pigs. The wise pig heard the animal on the roof and put a large pot of water on the fire.

When the animal came down the opening, he fell into the hot water. He jumped out with a loud cry and ran away, never to return.

The three little pigs were safe at last in their strong stone house. They learned that hard work and smart choices lead to the best results.

From that day on, they all lived together happily in the house. The lazy pig decided he would work harder from then on.

He realized that if he had built his house better, he would not have lost it. The second pig also understood the value of effort and care.

They both agreed that their brother’s house was the best of all. It was strong, safe, and warm, just as they had hoped.

The wise pig was glad to share his home with his brothers. He knew that family and safety were more important than anything else.

One day, they talked about what had happened with the animal. The first pig said, 'I should have built my house with stone like you did.'

The second pig nodded and said, 'I would have been safer if I had too.' The wise pig smiled and said, 'It is never too late to learn and change.'

They decided to make their home even better together. They added more stone and made sure everything was strong and secure.

They planted a garden with flowers and vegetables around the house. It became a lovely place where they could relax and enjoy life.

Sometimes, they would sit outside and talk about their adventures. They laughed about how the animal had tried to trick them.

They were glad they had learned their lesson well. The wise pig said, 'If we had not worked hard, we would not be safe now.'

The other pigs agreed and felt proud of their home. They knew that their efforts had made a big difference in their lives.

One evening, as they sat by the fire, they heard a knock at the door. They looked at each other, wondering who it could be.

The wise pig went to the door and asked, 'Who is there?' A soft voice replied, 'It is just a lost traveler looking for shelter.'

The pigs decided to let the traveler in, as they knew how it felt to be alone. They gave him food and a warm place to sleep for the night.

The next morning, the traveler thanked them and went on his way. The pigs felt good about helping someone in need.

They realized that kindness was just as important as hard work. From that day on, they tried to help others whenever they could.

Their home became known as a place of warmth and safety. People from the village would often visit and enjoy their company.

The pigs were happy and content with their lives. They knew that they had made the right choices and built a good life.

They often thought about how things could have been different. If they had not worked hard, they might not have been so lucky.

The first pig said, 'I am glad we learned to build strong houses.' The second pig added, 'And I am glad we learned to work together.'

The wise pig smiled and said, 'Yes, and we must always remember these lessons.' They all agreed and lived happily ever after in their strong stone house.

### C1
The land stretched green and undulating beneath a sky so vast it seemed to breathe, its horizon softened by the haze of late summer. Here, where the river curled like a silver ribbon through meadows of wild thyme and clover, three little pigs had lived all their lives in the shelter of their mother's sturdy oak-framed cottage. Now, with the first chill of autumn whispering through the leaves, they knew it was time to build homes of their own-not merely for warmth, but for the quiet dignity of independence.

The eldest, whose name was Bram, had always been the dreamer, his mind full of lazy afternoons and the rustle of dry grass beneath his trotters. He wanted a house that would rise quickly, like a thought, so he could spend his days lounging in the sun, watching clouds drift by without a care. So he gathered armfuls of golden straw from the fields, weaving them into walls that shimmered in the light but whispered secrets of fragility when the wind blew hard. When it was done, he stood back, grinning, and declared, 'This will do just fine-no need to fuss over stones when the sun is warm and the days are long.'

The middle brother, named Alder, was neither as reckless as Bram nor as patient as their youngest sibling, but he prided himself on being practical. He chose sticks-not the brittle kind that snapped underfoot, but sturdy branches from the ash trees, lashed together with vines until they formed a frame that creaked but held. It was work enough to tire him, and when he finished, he wiped his brow and muttered, 'This will stand against the wind, at least. No need to waste time on what won't be seen.' Yet even as he spoke, a doubt flickered in his chest, like a shadow passing over water.

The youngest, called Thorne, had always been different-quiet where his brothers were loud, still where they were restless. He had watched the masons in the village build their chimneys and walls, noting how each stone was chosen not for its beauty, but for its strength, how each one bore the weight of the next without complaint. So he began his own work, hauling stones from the riverbed, their surfaces smooth and cool beneath his paws, arranging them with care until the walls rose like a promise. Days turned to weeks, and though his brothers teased him for his slowness, Thorne worked on, his heart steady, his resolve unshaken.

One evening, as the last light bled into twilight, a shadow fell across Bram's threshold. It was the wolf-not the scrawny, whimpering creature of children's tales, but a great, gaunt beast with eyes like embers and a voice that rumbled like distant thunder. He knocked, his claws tapping against the straw, and called out, 'Little pig, little pig, let me in.' Bram's blood turned to ice, but he forced a laugh and shouted back, 'Not by the hair on my chinny-chin-chin!'-though his voice wavered like a reed in the wind.

The wolf did not hesitate. He drew in a breath so deep his ribs seemed to split, and then he blew-once, twice-and the straw house dissolved like mist before dawn. Bram barely had time to scream before he was running, his trotters kicking up dust as he fled toward Alder's house, the wolf's laughter echoing behind him like a curse. Alder, hearing the commotion, barred the door just in time, but the wolf was already there, his breath hot against the wood as he demanded entry. Again, the pigs refused, and again, the wolf blew-harder this time-and the sticks splintered like kindling, sending both brothers scrambling into the night.

They ran until their lungs burned, until the only sound was the ragged rhythm of their breath and the distant, relentless padding of the wolf's paws behind them. At last, they reached Thorne's house, its stone walls glowing warmly in the moonlight, and threw themselves inside, bolting the heavy oak door behind them. The wolf arrived moments later, his muzzle pressed against the cracks in the doorframe, his voice a low, mocking growl. 'Little pigs, little pigs, let me in.' Thorne, his heart pounding but his voice steady, replied, 'Not by the hair on our chinny-chin-chins.'

The wolf blew then-once, twice, three times-his breath a gale that howled around the house, rattling the shutters and shaking the very foundations. But the stones did not yield. They stood firm, as if they had been waiting for this moment all along, and the wolf's fury turned to frustration, then to cunning. He stepped back, his lips curling into a smile that was all teeth. 'Little pigs,' he called, 'I know a field where the roots are sweet as honey. Meet me there at dawn, and I'll show you.' The pigs exchanged glances, and though Thorne's eyes narrowed with suspicion, Bram and Alder, desperate for a way out, agreed.

That night, while the wolf slept beneath the stars, the pigs rose before the first light and hurried to the field, filling their baskets with the sweetest roots before vanishing back into the safety of Thorne's house. When the wolf arrived, his stomach growling with hunger, he found the field picked clean, and his rage was terrible to behold. He stormed back to the stone house, his mind alight with vengeance, and climbed onto the roof, his claws scraping against the tiles as he searched for a way in. Thorne, listening to the sound of the wolf's movements above, felt a cold certainty settle in his chest. He knew what had to be done.

He built up the fire until the flames roared, and set a great iron pot of water to boil, its surface shivering with heat. Then, as the wolf slithered down the chimney, Thorne tipped the pot, and the scalding water met the beast's fur with a hiss like a thousand snakes. The wolf howled, a sound so raw it seemed to shake the very earth, and fled into the night, his tail between his legs, never to return. The pigs, shaken but unharmed, stood in the sudden silence, their breaths coming slow and deep, as if they had all been holding them for years.

In the days that followed, Bram and Alder did not speak of their shame, but it hung between them like a mist, thick and unspoken. They worked alongside Thorne, hauling stones and mixing mortar, their hands growing calloused but their spirits lighter with each passing day. They planted a garden-roses for beauty, carrots for sustenance, and lavender to scent the air-and when the first blooms appeared, Bram admitted, 'I should have built with stone from the start.' Alder, his voice quiet, added, 'And I should have listened to you, Thorne. A house is more than walls-it's a promise to ourselves.'

One evening, as the three sat by the fire, a knock came at the door, soft but insistent. Thorne rose and opened it to find a traveler, his cloak damp with rain, his face drawn with weariness. 'I am lost,' the stranger said, 'and the night is cold.' The pigs looked at one another, and without a word, they made room by the fire, offering bread and warmth and the simple kindness of shelter. As the traveler left the next morning, he turned and said, 'This house is more than stone-it is a home.' And the pigs, watching him go, knew he was right.

## st-0213 — A Retrieved Reformation
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
Jimmy works in a place for ten months. He opens money places with work things. The man in charge talks to Jimmy. He says Jimmy can leave early. Jimmy looks and says no. He says he never opens money places. The man lets Jimmy go that day.

Jimmy meets his friend Mike. Mike gives Jimmy his work things back. Jimmy is glad to have them. He starts work again. In three weeks he opens three money places. A group looks for a man. The man opens money places without lines.

Jimmy goes to a small town. The town is called Elmore. He walks into a money place. He wants a room to live in. He sees a young woman. She looks very good. She looks at Jimmy. Her name is Annabel Adams. She is the money man parent. Jimmy likes her very much.

Jimmy decides to stay in Elmore. He changes his name to Ralph Spencer. He opens a shop. The shop does well. He makes many friends. After one year Ralph and Annabel join. Everyone in town likes Ralph. He feels good and well.

One day a man comes to town. His name is Ben Price. He is a watch man. He knows Ralph is Jimmy. Ben watches Ralph. He does not take him away. Ralph hopes Ben will leave him free.

The next day Ralph goes to the money place. His family goes with him. Annabel family members go too. A little child plays near the money place. The money place is new. The child gets in. The door closes. No one can open it. The child might die.

Ralph looks at Annabel. He looks at the money place. He knows he can open it. But Ben will take him away. He takes out his work things. He works with speed. In ten minutes the money place opens. The child is free.

Ralph stands up. He sees Ben at the door. He thinks his new life is over. Ben looks at Ralph. Ben walks away. Ralph stays free.

### A2
Jimmy Valentine was in a big place for ten months. He opened boxes with special tools there.

The head man told Jimmy he could leave early. Jimmy had to be good. Jimmy said he never opened boxes.

The man let Jimmy go that afternoon. Outside, Jimmy met his old friend Mike.

Mike gave Jimmy his things back. Jimmy was happy to see them. He went to work right away.

In three weeks, he opened boxes in three towns. The police looked for a man. The man opened boxes without marks.

One day, Jimmy went to a small town. The town was called Elmore. He went into the bank to ask for a room.

At the door, he saw a beautiful girl. She was the bank manager’s daughter. Her name was Annabel Adams.

Jimmy loved her smile. He wanted to stay in Elmore. Jimmy changed his name to Ralph Spencer.

He opened a shop for feet clothes. The shop did well. He made many friends.

After a year, Ralph asked Annabel to marry him. Everyone in town liked Ralph. He felt happy and quite good now.

One day, a police officer came to town. His name was Ben Price. Ben knew Ralph was really Jimmy.

Ben watched Ralph but did not take him. Ralph saw Ben in town. He hoped Ben would leave him alone.

He wanted to live his new life with Annabel. The next morning, Ralph went to the bank with family.

Annabel’s little child played near the box. The box was new and very strong. Suddenly, the child was inside the box.

The door closed by bad chance. No one could open it. Everyone felt very bad.

Ralph knew he could open the box. He looked at Annabel. He knew Ben would take him.

But he could not let the child die. Ralph took out his old tools. He worked fast.

In ten minutes, the box was open. The child was free. Ralph stood up and saw Ben.

He thought his new life was over. Ben looked at him and said nothing. Then Ben turned and walked away.

Ben did not take Ralph. Ralph was free. He passed his last test. He changed for love.

### B1
Jimmy was in prison for ten months. He opened strong boxes with special tools.

The prison boss told Jimmy he could leave early. He had to be good to get out.

Jimmy smiled and said he never opened boxes. That same day, the boss let Jimmy go.

Outside, Jimmy met his old friend Mike Dolan. Mike gave Jimmy his tools back.

Jimmy was happy to have his tools again. He went to work right away.

In less than three weeks, he opened boxes in three towns. The police started to look for him.

One day, Jimmy went to a small town called Elmore. He walked into the bank to ask about a room.

At the door, he saw a beautiful young woman. She was the bank owner’s child, Annabel.

Jimmy fell in love with her when he saw her smile. He decided to stay in Elmore.

Jimmy changed his name to Ralph Spencer. He opened a shoe shop in town.

The shop did very well, and he made many friends. After a year, Ralph was going to marry Annabel.

Everyone in Elmore liked Ralph. He felt happy and safe there.

One day, a man named Ben Price came to town. He knew Ralph was really Jimmy.

Ben watched Ralph but did not take him to prison. Ralph hoped Ben would leave him alone.

The next morning, Ralph and Annabel’s family went to the bank. Annabel’s little cousin May played near the new strong box.

Suddenly, May got shut inside the strong box. No one could open the special lock.

Ralph knew he was the only one who could save her. He looked at Annabel and then at the box.

If he opened it, Ben would take him to prison. But he could not let the child die.

Ralph took out his old tools and worked fast. In less than ten minutes, the box was open.

May was free, but Ralph saw Ben at the door. He knew his new life was over.

Ben looked at him and said, 'I do not know you, Mr. Spencer.' Then he turned and walked away.

Ralph stayed free because he saved the child. He had truly changed for love.

Jimmy had always been good with tools. He never thought he would use them to save a life.

Before he met Annabel, he only thought about himself. Now, he wanted to be a better man.

The people in Elmore did not know his past. They saw him as a kind and hard-working man.

Ralph worked hard every day in his shoe shop. He wanted to make Annabel proud.

He often thought about how his life had changed. He was happy and did not want to lose what he had.

When Ben Price came to town, Ralph felt worried. He knew Ben would not forget about him.

Ralph hoped he could keep his new life. He did not want to go back to prison.

The day May got shut in the box was very scary. Everyone in the bank tried to help her.

Annabel was very upset and cried for help. Ralph knew he had to do something.

He remembered the tools in his bag. He had not used them in a long time.

Ralph worked quickly and carefully. He did not want to make a mistake.

When the box opened, everyone was happy. May was safe and did not seem hurt.

Ralph looked at Ben and waited for him to act. He did not know what Ben would do.

Ben had seen everything. He knew Ralph was the one who opened the box.

But Ben did not take Ralph to prison. He walked away and left Ralph free.

Ralph was surprised but very happy. He could stay with Annabel and his new life.

From that day, Ralph worked even harder. He wanted to be the best man he could be.

Annabel loved him more than ever. She was proud of the man he had become.

The people in Elmore talked about what happened. They thought Ralph was very brave.

Ralph knew he had made the right choice. He would never go back to his old ways.

He had a new life and a new love. He would do anything to protect them.

### B2
Jimmy Valentine worked in a prison for ten months. He was there because he opened safes with special tools. The boss told Jimmy he could leave early if he behaved well. Jimmy just smiled and said he had never opened any safes. That same afternoon, the boss let Jimmy go free.

Outside the prison, Jimmy met his old friend Mike Dolan. Mike gave Jimmy his tools back, which he had kept safe. Jimmy was happy to have them again and went straight to work. In less than three weeks, he opened safes in three different towns. The police started to look for a man who opened safes without leaving marks. Many people were very upset when their money disappeared from the bank. The police searched everywhere, but they could not find any evidence or clues. Jimmy traveled from place to place and continued his old life. He felt that nothing could stop him because his work was too good.

One day, Jimmy arrived in a small town called Elmore. He walked into the bank to ask about a room to rent. As he stood at the door, he saw a beautiful young woman. She was the bank owner’s daughter, and her name was Annabel Adams. Jimmy fell in love with her the moment he saw her smile. He knew that his old life had to end if he wanted to win her heart.

Jimmy decided to stay in Elmore and start a new life. He changed his name to Ralph Spencer and opened a shoe shop. The shop did very well, and he made many friends in town. After a year, Jimmy, who was now Ralph, was engaged to Annabel. Everyone in Elmore liked Ralph, and he felt happy and safe. He made shoes for children and adults who lived in the community. His business grew larger each month, and he earned a good name. Annabel loved him deeply, and her family welcomed him into their home. Ralph often thought about his past, but he kept his secrets hidden very well.

One day, an officer named Ben Price arrived in town. He had followed Jimmy’s old crimes and knew who Ralph really was. Ben watched Ralph closely but did not arrest him right away. Ralph knew the officer was there, but he hoped Ben would leave him alone. He wanted to live his new life with Annabel and forget his past. Every time Ralph saw Ben on the street, his heart beat very fast. He wondered if the police officer would take him back to prison. However, Ben only watched and waited to see what Ralph would do next.

The next morning, Ralph and Annabel’s family went to the bank. Annabel’s little relative, May, was playing near the bank’s new safe. Suddenly, May got trapped inside the safe by accident. Everyone became afraid because the safe had a special lock no one could open. Ralph knew he was the only one who could save the child. Annabel cried out for help while her mother held her hands. The bank manager tried to turn the handle, but the heavy door remained shut. Time was running out, and the little girl needed air inside the dark space.

Ralph looked at Annabel and then at the safe. He knew if he opened it, Ben Price would arrest him. But he also knew he could not let the child die. He took out his old tools and worked quickly. In less than ten minutes, the safe was open, and May was free. Everyone cheered and thanked Ralph for his amazing action. Annabel hugged her little niece and smiled through her tears.

Ralph stood up and saw Ben Price standing at the door. He knew his new life was over, and he was ready to go back to prison. But Ben just looked at him and said, "I don’t know you, Mr. Spencer." Then he turned and walked away, leaving Ralph free. Ralph had passed his final test and truly changed for love. He looked at Annabel, knowing that they would share a wonderful future together. His old crimes were finally forgotten, and he was a free man at last.

### C1
For ten months, Jimmy Valentine resided within the stone walls of the state penitentiary, a place where time was measured in the heavy slamming of iron doors. His incarceration was the direct consequence of an extraordinary talent for manipulating complex safe locks with a specialized set of steel tools. Summoned before the warden on a crisp morning, Jimmy was informed that influential friends had secured an early release for exemplary behavior. With a disarming smile that reached his eyes, the young man calmly denied ever having possessed such tools or opened a single vault. That very afternoon, however, the administrative formalities were concluded, and the iron gates swung outward to restore his liberty.

Outside the grim perimeter, his old associate Mike Dolan waited to facilitate his immediate return to familiar routines. In the back room of a modest establishment, Mike handed over the leather case containing the meticulously maintained instruments of Jimmy's trade. A rush of familiar satisfaction washed over Jimmy as his fingers closed around the polished steel, and he resolved to waste no time. Within the span of less than three weeks, vaults in three distinct towns were cracked with a precision that left investigators entirely baffled. Not a single scratch marred the expensive lacquer, and local authorities found themselves utterly devoid of physical evidence or viable suspects.

Moving swiftly from one jurisdiction to another, Jimmy believed his craft had reached a level of perfection that rendered him untouchable. Fate, however, often introduces an unexpected variable just as a man feels most secure in his chosen path. It was during a journey through a quiet agricultural region that he stepped into the unassuming town of Elmore. Seeking a quiet place to establish a temporary base, he crossed the threshold of the local bank to inquire about residential rentals. As he paused near the heavy entrance, his gaze collided with that of a young woman whose grace instantly halted his breath.

She was Annabel Adams, the daughter of the institution's primary owner, and her radiant smile struck Jimmy with the force of a revelation. In that single, suspended heartbeat, the foundations of his adventurous, lawless existence crumbled into absolute insignificance. He understood with sudden clarity that the continuation of his criminal career meant the permanent forfeiture of any hope with her. Prompted by a powerful desire for redemption, Jimmy resolved to abandon his past entirely and plant his roots right there in Elmore. He adopted the alias Ralph Spencer and began laying the groundwork for a respectable, honest livelihood.

Utilizing capital saved from his earlier exploits, he established a high-end shoe emporium that quickly flourished under his diligent management. Ralph proved to be not only a capable merchant but a remarkably amiable neighbor, earning the genuine respect of the community. A year slipped away in a blur of peaceful routine, punctuated by pleasant evenings in the company of Annabel and her welcoming family. He crafted footwear for children and adults alike, embedding himself deeply into the social fabric of the thriving town. His business expanded steadily month by month, bringing him an unblemished reputation and the deep, abiding love of Annabel.

Though he frequently reflected upon the shadows of his former life, he kept those dangerous secrets locked away behind a charming demeanor. Then, on a Tuesday morning that began like any other, the fragile peace of his new existence was threatened by a familiar presence. An experienced detective named Ben Price, who had doggedly tracked Jimmy's previous heists, stepped off the midday stage. Recognizing Ralph instantly beneath his respectable exterior, the officer began observing the shoemaker from a cautious distance. Ralph felt his pulse race whenever he caught sight of the tall detective on the opposite side of the dusty main street.

He tormented himself with the persistent fear that handcuffs would soon replace the comfortable tools of his legitimate trade. Ben Price, however, made no aggressive moves, choosing instead to watch and evaluate the apparent transformation of the fugitive. The tension in Ralph's chest grew heavier with each passing day, though he maintained his outward composure for Annabel's sake. He dared to hope that his months of honest living might outweigh the sins written in police ledgers across three states. Life in Elmore continued its gentle rhythm, masking the silent drama playing out between the shoemaker and the patient lawman.

The ultimate test of Ralph Spencer's new identity arrived on a bright morning when he accompanied Annabel and her relatives to the bank. The establishment had recently installed a modern vault with a sophisticated time lock that was the pride of the manager. While the adults conversed near the front counter, a little girl named May began playing near the heavy steel door. In a swift, careless moment, the child slipped inside the cavernous interior and the massive mechanism clicked shut. Panic erupted instantly among the family members as they realized May was trapped in a dark, airless enclosure.

Annabel wept hysterically, clutching her mother's hands while the bank manager frantically spun the useless combination dial in vain. Time was evaporating rapidly, and the oxygen inside the heavy vault was already growing dangerously thin for the terrified child. Ralph stepped forward, his eyes locking onto Annabel's tear-streaked face before shifting to the impenetrable steel barrier. He understood precisely what the consequences would be if he chose to act, yet hesitation was no longer an option. Without uttering a single word of explanation, he reached into his coat and produced the polished steel instruments of his past.

Working with astonishing speed and absolute concentration, he manipulated the intricate tumblers while the onlookers watched in stunned silence. In less than ten minutes, the heavy door swung outward on oiled hinges, and little May tumbled safely into her mother's arms. Cheers echoed through the banking hall as the grateful family surrounded the shoemaker with tears of relief and overflowing gratitude. Ralph did not linger to accept their prolonged praise, but instead turned toward the front entrance with heavy resignation. He saw Ben Price standing near the door, and he braced himself for the inevitable cold click of steel bracelets.

To his utter astonishment, the detective looked straight through him, turned sharply on his heel, and walked out into the street. Ben Price muttered a quiet phrase denying any knowledge of a man named Ralph Spencer, leaving the bewildered shoemaker completely free. Ralph let out a long breath, realizing that his genuine transformation had been recognized and silently pardoned by the law. He looked across the room at Annabel, knowing that their shared future was now entirely secure and untainted by secrets. The ghost of Jimmy Valentine was finally laid to rest, leaving only an honest man standing in the warm morning light.

## st-0214 — Mammon and the Archer
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
Richard lives in New York. His father has a big business. His name is Anthony Rockwall. Anthony has much money.

Richard likes a girl. Her name is Miss Lantry. Her family is good. They have little money now.

Richard wants to ask Miss Lantry. He feels not good. He thinks she may say no. His family has much money.

Richard thinks of another man. The man’s name is Kelly. Kelly is from her town. Richard does not like Kelly.

One day, Richard talks to his father. He says Kelly may ask first. His father says money can help. Richard does not think so.

Anthony has a plan. He calls a man named Archer. Archer works for him. Archer is good at his job.

Anthony says stop all cars. Archer says he will try. The next day is a party. Richard will meet Miss Lantry there.

Anthony wants Richard to be on time. He tells Archer to stop cars. Archer does not know why. He starts his work.

Richard is in a car. The city is full. The car moves slow. Richard feels not good.

He thinks Kelly may ask first. Then all cars stop. Richard’s car stops too. He does not wait.

He gets out of the car. He starts to run. He runs through the city. He reaches the party.

Miss Lantry is there. No one has asked her. Richard goes to her. He says I love you.

He asks will you be with me. Miss Lantry says yes. Richard is very glad. He thanks his father later.

Anthony says money can do much. Richard knows it is true. People talk about the cars. No one knows why they stopped.

Richard and Miss Lantry live well. That is the end.

### A2
Richard Rockwall lived in New York. His father, Anthony, had a big business.

Anthony had a lot of money. Richard loved a girl named Miss Lantry.

Miss Lantry’s family was good but poor now. Richard wanted to marry her.

He thought she might say no. He thought she might not like his family.

Another man, Kelly, also liked her. Richard did not like Kelly.

Richard told his father about his problem. He said, 'If I don’t ask her soon, Kelly will.'

Anthony laughed and said, 'Money can fix any problem.' Richard did not think money could help love.

Anthony had a brother. The brother had a lot of money but did not like Anthony.

The brother had a son named Tom. Tom was not a good man.

The brother died last year. He left all his money to Tom.

Tom did not have the money yet. Anthony wanted to help Richard.

He made a plan to let Richard ask Miss Lantry first. Anthony called a man named Archer.

Archer worked for him and was good with money. Anthony said, 'Stop all the cars and buses in New York.'

He said, 'Do this for one hour.' Archer was surprised but said he would try.

The next day, Richard was going to a party. He was going to meet Miss Lantry there.

Anthony wanted Richard to get there on time. He told Archer to stop all cars and buses.

Richard was in a car on the way to the party. The streets were full, and the car did not move fast.

Richard worried he would be late. He thought, 'If I am late, Kelly will ask her first.'

Suddenly, all the cars and buses stopped. People could not move.

Richard’s car stopped too. He did not wait and got out.

He started to run to the party. He ran fast through the streets.

No cars or buses were there. Richard reached the party on time.

Miss Lantry was there, and no one asked her yet. He went to her and said, 'I love you.'

He asked, 'Will you marry me?' Miss Lantry smiled and said yes.

Richard was very happy. Later, he thanked his father.

Anthony laughed and said, 'Money can do anything.' Richard knew his father was right.

It worked well. The next day, people talked about the stop.

No one knew why it happened. Archer never told anyone about the plan.

Richard and Miss Lantry lived happily.

### B1
Richard Rockwall lived in New York. He was a young man.

His father, Anthony Rockwall, had a large business. He had a lot of money.

Richard loved a girl named Miss Lantry. She came from a good family.

But her family had little money now. Richard wanted to marry her.

He was afraid she might say no. He thought it was because his family was rich.

He also worried about another man named Kelly. Kelly was from her town.

Richard did not like Kelly. He thought Kelly might ask her first.

One day, Richard told his father about his problem. He said, 'If I do not ask her soon, Kelly will ask her first.'

His father laughed and said, 'Money can solve any problem.' Richard did not believe money could help with love.

Anthony had a brother before. This brother was very rich too.

But he did not like Anthony. The brother had a son named Tom.

Tom was not a good man. Richard did not like him either.

Richard’s brother died the year before. He left all his money to Tom.

But Tom did not get the money yet. Anthony wanted to use this to help Richard.

He thought of a plan. He wanted to make sure Richard could ask Miss Lantry first.

Anthony called a man named Archer. Archer worked for him.

Archer was very good at solving problems with money. Anthony said, 'I need you to stop the traffic in New York for one hour.'

Archer was surprised but said he would try. The next day, Richard was to meet Miss Lantry at a party.

Anthony wanted to make sure Richard got there on time. He told Archer to stop all the cars, buses, and trains.

Archer did not know why but he started his work. At the same time, Richard was in a taxi.

He was on his way to the party. The streets were very busy.

The taxi could not move fast. Richard was worried he would be late.

He thought, 'If I am late, Kelly will ask her first.' Suddenly, all the traffic stopped.

Cars, buses, and people could not move at all. Richard’s taxi was stuck too.

But he did not wait. He got out and started to run.

He ran as fast as he could through the empty streets. He reached the party just in time.

Miss Lantry was there. No one else had asked her yet.

He went to her and said, 'I love you.' 'Will you marry me?' he asked.

Miss Lantry smiled and said yes. Richard was very happy.

He thanked his father later. Anthony laughed and said, 'Money can do anything, even help love.'

Richard knew his father was right. He was glad it worked.

The next day, people talked about the strange traffic stop. No one knew why it happened.

But it helped Richard. Archer never told anyone about his part in the plan.

Richard and Miss Lantry lived happily after that. They got married soon.

Richard started to work in his father’s business. He learned a lot about money and plans.

Miss Lantry helped him see love was important too. They often talked about their future together.

Anthony was happy his plan worked. He was glad his son found love.

Kelly never got a chance to ask Miss Lantry. He left town soon after.

Tom finally got his money but it did not make him happy. He spent it all on bad things.

Richard and Miss Lantry had a big party for their wedding. Many people came to celebrate.

They lived in a nice house in New York. They had two children after a few years.

Richard’s father visited them often. He loved his grandchildren very much.

Miss Lantry’s family was happy too. They had little money but a lot of love.

Richard always remembered how he ran through the empty streets. It was the day he found his true love.

He often told the story to his children. They loved to hear about their parents’ love.

The traffic stop became a funny family story. Everyone laughed when they heard it.

Richard was glad his father helped him. He knew love was the most important thing.

### B2
Richard Rockwall was a young man who lived in New York City.

His father, Anthony Rockwall, had a large business and a lot of money.

Richard loved a girl named Miss Lantry.

She came from a good family, but her family had little money now.

Richard wanted to marry Miss Lantry, but he felt afraid.

He thought she might not say yes because his family was very rich.

He also worried that another man, named Kelly, might ask her first.

Kelly was a young man from her town, and Richard did not like him.

One day, Richard told his father about his problem.

He said, 'If I do not ask her soon, Kelly will ask her before me.'

His father laughed and said, 'Money can solve any problem you have.'

Richard did not believe that money could help with love.

Anthony Rockwall had a brother who was Richard’s uncle.

This uncle was very rich too, but he did not like Anthony.

The uncle had a son named Tom, who was Richard’s cousin.

Tom was not a good man, and Richard did not like him either.

Richard’s uncle had died the year before.

He left all his money to Tom, but Tom did not get it yet.

Anthony wanted to use this to help Richard.

He thought of a plan to make sure Richard could ask Miss Lantry first.

Anthony called a man named Archer, who worked for him.

Archer was very good at solving problems with money.

Anthony said, 'I need you to stop the traffic in New York for one hour.'

Archer was surprised but said he would try.

The next day, Richard was to meet Miss Lantry at a party.

Anthony wanted to make sure Richard got there on time.

He told Archer to stop all the cars, buses, and trains in the city.

Archer did not know why, but he started his work.

At the same time, Richard was in a taxi on his way to the party.

The streets were very busy, and the taxi could not move fast.

Richard was worried he would be late.

He thought, 'If I am late, Kelly will ask her first.'

Suddenly, all the traffic stopped.

Cars, buses, and people could not move at all.

Richard’s taxi was stuck too, but he did not wait.

He got out and started to run to the party.

Richard ran as fast as he could through the empty streets.

He reached the party just in time.

Miss Lantry was there, and no one else had asked her yet.

He went to her and said, 'I love you. Will you marry me?'

Miss Lantry smiled and said yes.

Richard was very happy, and he thanked his father later.

Anthony laughed and said, 'Money can do anything, even help love.'

Richard knew his father was right, but he was glad it worked.

The next day, people talked about the strange traffic stop.

No one knew why it happened, but it helped Richard a lot.

Archer never told anyone about his part in the plan.

Richard and Miss Lantry lived happily after that.

That was the end of their story, but it was just the start of their life together.

Richard often thought about how his father had helped him.

He knew that without his father’s plan, he might not have asked Miss Lantry in time.

Miss Lantry was glad Richard had come to the party early.

She had waited for him and hoped he would ask her to marry him.

Kelly had not arrived yet, so Richard had the chance he wanted.

Richard’s father was proud of what he had done.

He believed that money could solve many problems in life.

Richard did not always agree, but he was happy his father had helped.

The traffic stop was a strange thing to happen in the city.

People talked about it for days after it occurred.

Some thought it was a mistake, but others knew it was not.

Archer had done his job well, and no one found out why.

Richard and Miss Lantry had a big wedding a few months later.

Many people came to celebrate with them.

They were a happy couple, and everyone could see it.

Richard’s father gave them a nice house as a wedding gift.

He said, 'This is for you both, so you can start your life together.'

Richard and Miss Lantry thanked him with a big smile.

They knew they would be happy in their new home.

Kelly was not at the wedding, and no one missed him.

He had left town soon after the party.

Richard was glad he did not have to see him again.

Miss Lantry was happy too, because she did not like Kelly.

She had always hoped Richard would ask her to marry him.

Now that he had, she felt like the luckiest girl in the world.

Richard felt the same way about her.

He knew he had found the right person to spend his life with.

Their love was strong, and they wanted to be together always.

Anthony Rockwall was happy for his son.

He had wanted Richard to find a good wife, and he had.

Miss Lantry was kind and smart, and Anthony liked her very much.

He thought she would make a great wife for his son.

Richard and Miss Lantry often visited Anthony after they married.

He enjoyed their company and loved to hear about their life.

They told him about their plans and their dreams for the future.

Anthony always listened and gave them good advice.

He was glad he could help them, even if it was just with money.

Richard knew his father loved him, and that was what mattered most.

Miss Lantry loved Anthony too, because he was kind to her.

She was happy to have him as a father-in-law.

Together, they were a happy family, and life was good.

### C1
The autumn air in New York carried the crisp scent of fallen leaves and distant chimney smoke, a reminder that the city's relentless energy could still pause for something as fragile as a season. Richard Rockwall stood at the window of his father's Fifth Avenue mansion, watching the golden light spill across the pavement, his reflection a pale shadow against the glass. He had spent the last three months rehearsing the words-simple, direct-but now that the moment approached, they dissolved like sugar in rain. Miss Lantry would be at the Astor ball that evening, and if he did not speak before midnight, he feared another man would claim the space beside her first.

His father, Anthony Rockwall, sat in a leather armchair by the fire, the glow of the flames flickering across his sharp features. A self-made man who had clawed his way from the docks to the boardroom, he had little patience for hesitation, especially when it came to matters of the heart-or, as he preferred to call them, matters of strategy. 'You're overthinking it,' he said, not looking up from the ledger in his lap. 'A woman like Miss Lantry doesn't care about the size of your bank account. She cares about the size of your spine.'

Richard turned away from the window, his fingers tightening around the velvet curtain. It wasn't the money that troubled him-though God knew his father's fortune cast a long, distorting shadow-but the gnawing certainty that he was already too late. Kelly, that smirking, silver-tongued son of a local judge, had been circling Miss Lantry for weeks, his intentions as transparent as they were unwelcome. Richard had seen the way Kelly's eyes lingered on her at the opera, the way his laughter rose just a fraction too loud when she spoke.

Anthony snapped the ledger shut and rose, his movements deliberate, like a man who had never once doubted his own power. 'Money can't buy love,' Richard muttered, 'but it can buy time.' His father's lips curled into something between a smile and a sneer. 'Time is the only thing money *can* buy. The rest is just noise.' He crossed the room and clapped a hand on Richard's shoulder, his grip firm, almost bruising. 'Leave it to me. By tonight, you'll have your chance.'

The plan, when Anthony laid it out, was absurd in its simplicity. His brother-Richard's uncle, a man who had despised Anthony to his dying breath-had left his fortune to his wastrel son, Tom, but the funds were tied up in legal knots until the new year. Anthony had spent the last decade ensuring those knots would never fully unravel, and now, with a single phone call, he intended to exploit them. He summoned Archer, a man whose name alone made clerks and policemen alike straighten in their chairs, and gave him an order: stop the city for one hour.

Archer did not ask why. He had long since learned that Anthony Rockwall's whims were not to be questioned, only executed with the precision of a surgeon's blade. By noon the next day, the gears of New York's transit system began to grind to a halt-not with the chaos of an accident, but with the eerie stillness of a held breath. Trains idled on their tracks, buses stood motionless in the streets, and the usual cacophony of horns and shouts faded into an unsettling silence.

Richard, meanwhile, was trapped in a taxi on Broadway, the driver's curses growing increasingly inventive as the minutes ticked past. He checked his watch for the tenth time in as many minutes, his pulse hammering against his ribs like a prisoner demanding release. If he was late, Kelly would be there, smooth and smiling, and Miss Lantry-kind, patient Miss Lantry-might mistake politeness for affection. The thought sent a cold spike through his chest, and without another word, he flung open the door and began to run.

The streets were a ghost town, the usual crush of bodies and vehicles replaced by an unnatural emptiness. Richard's shoes slapped against the pavement, the sound echoing off the brick facades like a metronome counting down the seconds he had left. He dodged a stalled delivery cart, vaulted over a fallen newspaper stand, and skidded around a corner, his lungs burning, his coat flapping behind him like a flag of surrender. When he finally burst into the Astor's grand foyer, his breath came in ragged gasps, his hair disheveled, his collar damp with sweat.

Miss Lantry stood near the grand staircase, her gloved hands clasped lightly before her, her dark eyes scanning the room with quiet amusement. She turned as he approached, her expression shifting from curiosity to something warmer, something that made his throat tighten. Kelly was nowhere to be seen-perhaps still stuck in the same frozen traffic, perhaps simply late, perhaps, for once, outmaneuvered. Richard dropped to one knee before he could second-guess himself, the marble floor cold beneath his palm as he took her hand in his.

'I love you,' he said, the words rough but sure, 'and I cannot imagine my life without you in it.' Miss Lantry's lips parted, her fingers trembling in his grip, and for a heartbeat, the noise of the ballroom-the clink of glasses, the murmur of conversation-faded into silence. Then she smiled, slow and radiant, and said, 'I've been waiting for you to ask.' Around them, the world resumed its motion, but Richard barely noticed, his heart too full to care about anything but the warmth of her hand in his.

The next morning, the newspapers buzzed with theories about the city's sudden paralysis, but no one connected it to the Rockwall fortune or the quiet engagement announced in the society pages. Anthony read the announcement over breakfast, his satisfaction as subtle as a cat's purr, while Richard, still floating on the memory of Miss Lantry's smile, barely tasted his coffee. Archer, ever discreet, vanished into the city's underbelly, his role in the affair known only to the two men who had hired him-and who would never speak of it again.

Three months later, under a canopy of roses in the Rockwall garden, Richard and Miss Lantry exchanged vows, their voices steady, their hands clasped as if they had never once doubted the outcome. Anthony watched from the front row, his chest puffed with pride, though he would never admit that his son's happiness meant more to him than any business deal. As the newlyweds danced their first waltz, Kelly-who had not been invited-lingered on the edge of the crowd, his face a mask of bitter resignation. Richard caught his eye and smiled, not with triumph, but with the quiet certainty of a man who had already won everything that mattered.

---

## st-0215 — Witches' Loaves
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
A woman has a small shop. Her shop sells food.

People like her shop. The food is good and new.

The woman is kind. She talks to people a lot.

A man comes to the shop every day. He has the same old coat.

He buys two things of food. He does not buy more food.

The woman thinks about him. She wants to help him.

One day, she sees color on his hand. The color is not black.

She remembers he carries a big book. She thinks he may draw.

She decides to make his food better. She puts something in the food.

The next day, he comes in. She gives him the food.

She does not say anything. She hopes he will like it.

The man takes the food and goes. He says nothing.

The next day, he comes back. He has a friend with him.

He does not look well. He speaks in a big way.

He shows her the food. He says he uses food for work.

The food takes away color from paper. The thing she put makes his work bad.

He tells her not to do it again. Then he goes away.

The woman stands still. Her face turns white.

She feels bad about it. She knows she did a bad thing.

The man never comes back. She learns from this.

She will not do the same thing again. She wants to keep her customers.

### A2
Miss Martha had a small shop in the town center. She sold food every day.

People liked her shop. The food was always good. Miss Martha was kind. She often talked to her customers.

One man came to the shop almost every day. He was not big and wore eye covers. He always wore the same old clothes.

He never bought anything but two old pieces of bread. Miss Martha felt sorry for him. He looked poor and alone.

She wanted to help him. She thought about him often. One day she saw blue color on his hands.

She remembered he always carried a big book. Miss Martha thought he might draw pictures. People who draw pictures use color.

She decided to make his old bread better. The next time he came she put fat inside. She did not tell him.

She hoped he would like it. She hoped he would feel happier. The man took the bread and left. He did not say anything.

The next day the man came back with a friend. He looked very unhappy. He spoke in a loud voice.

He showed Miss Martha the two pieces of bread. They had fat inside. He said he used the bread to take away marks from paper.

He used it for his pictures. Miss Martha felt bad. Her face turned red.

The man said old bread was best for his work. Her fat had hurt many hours of work. He told her never to do it again. Then he left fast.

Miss Martha stood still behind the table for a long time. She knew she did the wrong thing. She felt very sorry.

The man never came back to her shop after that day. She learned that good plans can sometimes cause trouble. She would be more careful in the future.

Miss Martha thought about the man often. She hoped he found another way to take away marks. She wanted to say sorry but he never returned.

Her shop was still busy every day. Many people came to buy food. She always talked to them in a kind way.

She remembered the lesson she learned. She would not change food again without asking. It was an important lesson for her.

Miss Martha looked at the table where the man used to stand. She felt a little sad but also wiser. She knew she would not make the same mistake again.

### B1
Miss Martha Meacham owned a small shop in the town center. She sold food every day.

People liked her shop. The food was always fresh and good. Miss Martha was kind. She often talked to the people who came in.

A man came to the shop almost every day. He was thin and wore glasses. He always wore the same old coat.

He never bought anything except two dry bits of bread. Miss Martha felt sorry for him. He looked poor and alone.

She wanted to help him. She thought about him often. One day, she saw blue color on his fingers.

She remembered he always carried a large book under his arm. Miss Martha thought he might draw pictures. People who draw pictures use color.

She decided to make his dry bread a little softer. The next time he came, she put soft food inside the bread. She did not tell him.

She hoped he would enjoy it and feel happier. The man took the bread and left. He did not say anything.

The next day, the man came back with a friend. He looked very angry. He spoke in a strong voice.

He showed Miss Martha the two bits of bread with soft food inside. He said he used the dry bread to remove marks from his pictures.

Dry bread worked well for his work. Her soft food had ruined many hours of careful work. He told her never to do it again.

He left quickly. Miss Martha stood still behind the table for a long time. She felt bad and her face turned red.

She realized she had made a mistake. She felt very sorry. The man never returned to her shop after that day.

She learned that good plans can sometimes cause trouble. She would remember this lesson for a long time.

Miss Martha still ran her shop well. She continued to sell good food to her customers. But she was more careful about what she did.

If someone seemed to need help, she would think first. She would ask herself if her help might cause a problem. Only then would she decide what to do.

The shop remained a nice place in the town center. People still came every day to buy food. Miss Martha met them with a smile, but she was wiser now.

She often thought about the man with the book. She wondered if he had finished his pictures. She hoped he was happy.

One morning, a young woman came into the shop. She carried a book like the man’s. Miss Martha looked at her carefully.

The young woman bought two dry bits of bread. Miss Martha did not put anything inside them. She simply gave them to her with a smile.

The young woman smiled back and left. Miss Martha felt good about what she had done. She knew she had learned from her mistake.

From that day on, Miss Martha was happy in her shop. She sold food and talked to her customers. She was kind, but she was also careful.

If she saw someone who needed help, she would think first. She would not act too quickly. She knew that good intentions could sometimes cause trouble.

One day, a customer asked if she had ever helped the man again. Miss Martha said she had not. She explained that she now understood the situation better.

The customer nodded and said it was a good lesson. Miss Martha agreed. She felt the lesson had made her a better person.

The shop stayed busy every day. People enjoyed coming to buy food. They liked talking to Miss Martha too.

She often told the story to new customers. She wanted them to learn from her mistake. She hoped it would help them in their own lives.

Miss Martha knew she would never forget the man. She hoped he was doing well. She wished him happiness wherever he was.

The shop continued to be a friendly place. Miss Martha made sure everything was always fresh. She took great care with every customer.

Sometimes, she would see someone with blue on their fingers. She would smile but say nothing. She knew they might be drawing pictures too.

Miss Martha had learned to watch and listen first. She would only help if it was the right thing to do. She was happy with the way she ran her shop now.

People in the town often spoke about her shop. They said it was one of the best places to buy food. They also said Miss Martha was one of the kindest people they knew.

She was proud of her shop and her customers. She knew she had made a difference in the town. She looked forward to every new day.

### B2
Miss Martha Meacham had a small shop in the town center. She sold many kinds of bread and cakes every day.

People liked her shop because the food was always fresh and good. Miss Martha was kind and often talked to her customers.

One man came to the shop nearly every day. He was thin and wore glasses and the same old coat.

He never bought anything except two pieces of dry bread. Miss Martha felt sad for him because he seemed poor and alone.

She wanted to help him and thought about him a lot. One day, she saw blue color on his fingers.

She remembered he always carried a big book under his arm. Miss Martha thought he might be someone who made pictures.

She decided to make his dry bread a little softer. The next time he came, she put some soft food inside the bread.

She did not tell him about it. She hoped he would enjoy it and feel a bit happier.

The man took the bread and left without saying anything. The next day, he came back with a friend.

He looked very upset and spoke in a loud voice. He showed Miss Martha the two pieces of bread with soft food inside.

He said he used the dry bread to take away marks from his pictures. The soft food had ruined many hours of his careful work.

Miss Martha felt very sorry and her face turned red. The man told her never to do it again and left quickly.

Miss Martha stood still behind the counter for a long time. She understood her mistake and felt very bad.

The man never returned to her shop after that day. She learned that even good wishes can sometimes bring trouble.

Miss Martha still thought about the man often. She wondered if he ever found another way to clean his pictures.

She also wondered if he was still alone or if he had friends now. Sometimes, she looked at the blue color on her own fingers.

She remembered how the man had carried his book every day. She thought about how important his work was to him.

She decided she would not try to help people in that way again. She would only give them what they asked for.

If she saw someone who seemed to need help, she would ask first. She would not assume she knew what was best for them.

Miss Martha hoped the man was doing well. She hoped he had found a way to keep making his pictures.

She also hoped he was not too angry with her. She had only wanted to do something nice for him.

One day, a woman came into the shop. She looked at the bread and then at Miss Martha.

The woman said she had heard about what happened. She said the man had told her the whole story.

Miss Martha felt her face get warm again. She thought the woman might be angry with her too.

But the woman smiled and said she understood. She said she had made similar mistakes before.

Miss Martha felt a little better after that. She realized that everyone makes mistakes sometimes.

The woman bought some bread and left. Miss Martha watched her go and thought about the man again.

She wondered if she would ever see him again. She hoped that if she did, she could say she was sorry.

Miss Martha decided to keep her shop open as usual. She would continue to sell bread and cakes to her customers.

She would be kind and talk to them as she always had. But she would remember the lesson she had learned.

She would not try to change what people asked for. She would give them exactly what they wanted.

If someone needed help, she would ask how she could help. She would not decide for them what was best.

Miss Martha felt that this was a better way to be kind. She hoped that in the future, her kindness would not cause trouble.

She looked around her shop and smiled. It was a good place, and she was glad to be there.

She thought about all the people who came in every day. She hoped they would continue to enjoy her bread and cakes.

She also hoped they would enjoy talking to her. She liked the conversations she had with her customers.

Miss Martha knew that her shop was an important part of the town. She wanted it to stay that way for a long time.

She would work hard to keep her shop nice and welcoming. She would make sure the food was always fresh and good.

She would be kind and listen to her customers. She would remember the lesson she had learned from the man.

She would not try to change what people wanted. She would give them what they asked for and nothing more.

If she saw someone who needed help, she would ask first. She would not assume she knew what was best for them.

Miss Martha felt that this was the right way to run her shop. She hoped it would help her avoid any more trouble.

She looked at the counter and the shelves behind it. Everything was in its place, and the shop looked nice.

She took a deep breath and smiled. It was going to be a good day.

### C1
The bell above the door of Miss Martha Meacham's bakery gave a soft, almost apologetic chime as the morning light slanted through the front window, gilding the glass jars of cinnamon sticks and the copper scales that had stood sentinel on the counter for thirty years. Inside, the air was thick with the warm, yeasty breath of rising dough and the faint, buttery scent of just-baked brioche-Miss Martha's specialty, the one that brought customers back even when the day was gray and their purses were thin. She moved behind the counter with the quiet efficiency of someone who had memorized every inch of the shop, her fingers brushing the crust of a loaf as if to check its readiness, though she already knew it was perfect. The shop itself was small but immaculate: shelves lined with sourdough boules, rye loaves dark as twilight, and trays of almond biscotti arranged in neat, golden rows. It was a place where the outside world-with its rush and noise-seemed to pause at the threshold, as if even the cobblestones outside understood that here, things moved at the pace of a well-kneaded dough.

Miss Martha had always believed that kindness was a kind of yeast, too-something that, if added in the right measure, could make even the plainest life rise a little. This was why, when the thin man with the glasses began appearing in her shop each afternoon, she found herself watching him not with the detached curiosity of a shopkeeper, but with the quiet concern of someone who had noticed a crack in a beloved teacup. He wore the same threadbare coat every day, its elbows worn to a dull shine, and though he never spoke beyond the brief, polite order-"Two loaves of the day-old white, please"-his silence was not the empty kind. It was the silence of someone who carried a world inside him, one that Miss Martha, in her small, hopeful way, longed to understand. She told herself it was only natural to wonder about him: the way his fingers, long and delicate, always brushed the crumbs from the counter into his palm before he left; the way his eyes lingered on the shelves for a moment too long, as if he were memorizing the shapes of the loaves rather than choosing between them.

Then, one Tuesday, she saw the blue stain. It was barely more than a smudge on the pad of his thumb, but it was unmistakable-the kind of color that came from mixing pigment, from dipping a brush into something rich and alive. Miss Martha felt a small, electric thrill run through her, the way it always did when a mystery revealed its first clue. The book he carried under his arm-always the same one, its spine cracked with use-was no ordinary book, she realized. It was a sketchbook, or perhaps a portfolio, and the man was not just a man, but an artist, one who worked in quiet, solitary hours, his hands busy with something far more precious than bread.

That evening, as she kneaded the dough for the next day's batch, her mind kept returning to him-not with pity, as it had before, but with a kind of reverence. She imagined him in a cramped attic room, the light slanting through a grimy window as he bent over his work, the blue of his fingers bleeding into the paper like the sky at dusk. The dry bread he bought was not for eating, she realized with a jolt; it was for something else, something she had seen once in a book about old techniques: artists used stale bread to lift charcoal or pencil from their drawings, to erase mistakes without smudging the lines beneath. The thought of it made her heart ache-not because he was poor, but because he was careful, because he valued his work enough to treat it with such precision. And here she was, selling him bread that was, to him, nothing more than a tool, a means to an end.

The next morning, she woke with a plan already forming, as if it had been waiting for her in the dark. She would not tell him, of course-some things were too delicate for words-but she would give him a gift, a small one, something to let him know that someone had seen him, had understood. She spent an extra hour in the kitchen, mixing a paste of butter and honey, just enough to soften the crust of the bread without making it soggy. When he came in that afternoon, his glasses fogged from the cold, she handed him the loaves with a smile that felt both shy and proud, as if she were presenting him with a secret. He took them without a word, his fingers brushing hers for the briefest moment, and left, the bell chiming softly behind him.

The next day, he did not come alone. Miss Martha was arranging a fresh batch of currant buns in the window when the door swung open with more force than usual, the bell jangling as if in protest. The thin man stood there, his face flushed, his glasses askew, and beside him was another man-broader, louder, his voice filling the shop before Miss Martha could even greet them. "You see?" the thin man said, thrusting the two loaves toward her, his hands trembling slightly. Miss Martha's stomach dropped as she saw the glistening smear of butter seeping through the crust, the honey darkening the bread in uneven patches.

The broader man leaned over the counter, his voice sharp with accusation. "Do you know what you've done?" Miss Martha's mouth went dry. "I-I only thought-" "You thought wrong," the thin man interrupted, his voice quieter but no less cutting. He opened the book he carried, and Miss Martha saw the pages-dozens of them, covered in intricate sketches, the lines precise and confident, the shading so delicate it seemed to breathe. But in the center of one page, there was a blot, a smear of blue where the bread had left its mark, ruining hours of work.

The thin man's hands were clenched at his sides, his knuckles white. "I use the bread to lift the graphite," he said, his voice tight with controlled fury. "It's the only thing that doesn't damage the paper. But now-" He gestured helplessly at the ruined sketch, his throat working as if the words were too heavy to speak. Miss Martha felt the heat rise in her cheeks, her fingers twisting in the folds of her apron. "I didn't know," she whispered. The broader man scoffed. "Of course you didn't. That's the problem." The thin man said nothing, but his silence was worse than anger-it was the silence of someone who had been disappointed one time too many, and had no words left to give.

They left as abruptly as they had come, the door slamming behind them, the bell giving a final, mournful chime. Miss Martha stood behind the counter, her breath shallow, her heart pounding as if she had run a great distance. The shop, which had always been a place of warmth and comfort, now felt too small, the air too thick, the scent of baking bread suddenly cloying. She thought of the ruined sketch, of the hours of work lost, and her stomach twisted with shame. It was not the mistake itself that hurt the most, but the look in his eyes-the way he had looked at her not with anger, but with something far worse: resignation.

For weeks after that, she waited, half-hoping he would return, half-dreading the moment he might. She told herself that if he did, she would apologize, would explain that she had only meant to offer a kindness, not to meddle. But the days passed, and he did not come, and slowly, the shop returned to its usual rhythm, the customers chattering as they always had, the bread selling as it always did. Yet something in Miss Martha had shifted, like a door that had been left ajar, letting in a draft she could not quite close. She found herself watching her customers more closely now, wondering not just what they wanted, but why they wanted it, what stories they carried with them that she might never know.

Then, one afternoon, a woman entered the shop-a stranger, her coat damp from the rain, her eyes sharp and assessing as they took in the shelves. Miss Martha recognized the look at once: it was the look of someone who had heard a story and come to judge for herself. The woman approached the counter, her gaze flicking to Miss Martha's hands, as if searching for traces of blue. "I heard about what happened," she said, her voice neutral. Miss Martha's face burned, but the woman only smiled, a small, knowing smile. "We've all done it," she said. "Tried to fix something that wasn't ours to fix." Miss Martha felt something inside her loosen, just a little, as if a knot she hadn't known was there had finally begun to unravel.

The woman bought a loaf of sourdough and left, the bell chiming softly behind her, but the encounter stayed with Miss Martha long after the shop had closed for the day. She thought of the thin man, of the blue on his fingers, of the way his book had felt like a door to another world, one she had tried-and failed-to open. She realized then that kindness was not about giving people what she thought they needed, but about listening, truly listening, to what they asked for. It was about respecting the quiet spaces between words, the things left unsaid, the tools that were not just tools, but extensions of the hands that wielded them. And so, when the next customer came in, she smiled and asked, "What can I get for you today?"-and meant it.
