## st-0216 — One Thousand Dollars
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
Gillian is a young man. He never works. He gets money from his father.

One day, Gillian gets a letter. The letter is from a man. The man works at a place.

The man says Gillian’s father dies. Gillian gets one thousand. He goes to the man’s place.

The man says it is a test. If Gillian uses the money well, he gets more. If he uses it badly, he gets nothing.

Gillian walks in the city. He sees many people. He buys some things for a woman.

She says thank you. Gillian feels the same. He gives money to a man. The man has no home.

The man says thank you. Gillian still feels nothing. He thinks of Miss Hayden.

She works for his father. Gillian likes her. He decides to give her the money.

He goes to her house. He goes to the door. She opens it. He gives her the money.

He says, ‘This is for you.’ He says she is kind to his father. She looks at him. She takes the money.

Gillian feels good. He goes back to the man. He tells the man what he does.

The man reads a note. The note says Gillian gets nothing if he uses money badly. He gets more if he uses it well.

The man asks Gillian to write it. Gillian writes how he uses it. He writes, ‘I give it to Miss Hayden.’

He writes she needs it more. The man says, ‘Your father would be happy.’

The man tells Gillian more. His father gives Miss Hayden one. The rest goes to Gillian.

But only if he uses the money well. Gillian passes the test. Now he will get much money.

### A2
Gillian was a young man. He never worked in his life.

His father’s brother was old. He often gave Gillian money.

One day, Gillian got a letter. It was from his father’s brother’s company.

The letter said his father’s brother died. He left Gillian one thousand dollars.

Gillian went to the company. A man there told him about a test.

The man said Gillian would get more money. But only if he used the money well.

If he did not use it well, the money would go. It would go to another person.

Gillian left the company. He thought about what to do.

He walked through the city. He saw many people there.

He bought some things for a woman. She smiled, but Gillian felt bad.

He gave money to a poor man on the street. The man said thank you, but Gillian still felt bad.

He thought about buying a car. But that did not seem right.

Then he thought of Miss Hayden. She worked for his father’s brother for many years.

Gillian liked her but never told her. He decided to give her the money.

He went to her house. He put his hand on the door.

Miss Hayden opened the door. Gillian gave her the money.

He said, ‘This is for you.’ ‘You were always kind to my father’s brother.’

Miss Hayden looked at him with big eyes. She took the money.

Gillian felt happy for the first time. He went back to the company.

He told the man what he did. The man read a note from Gillian’s father’s brother.

The note said if Gillian used the money well, he would get more. If not, he would get nothing.

The man asked Gillian to write how he used it. Gillian wrote, ‘I gave it to Miss Hayden.’

He wrote, ‘She needed it more than me.’ The man smiled.

He said, ‘Your father’s brother would be happy.’ Then he told Gillian the real thing.

His father’s brother left Miss Hayden one dollar. The rest of the money was for Gillian.

But only if he used the one thousand well. Gillian did well on the test.

Now he would have much money. He felt very happy about it.

### B1
Gillian was a young man. He had never had a job in his life.

A rich old man gave Gillian money when he asked. The old man was Gillian’s father’s brother.

One day, Gillian got a letter. It was from the old man’s lawyer.

The letter said the old man died. He left Gillian one thousand dollars.

Gillian went to the lawyer’s office the next morning. The lawyer told him about a test.

If Gillian used the money well, he would get fifty thousand dollars more. But if he used it badly, the money would go to another person.

Gillian left the office. He thought about what to do.

He walked through the city. He saw many people around him.

He saw a man selling flowers. Gillian bought some flowers for a woman he did not know.

The woman smiled. But Gillian did not feel happy.

He went to a restaurant. He saw a poor man outside.

Gillian gave the poor man some money. The man thanked him.

But Gillian still felt empty inside. He thought about buying something expensive for himself.

That did not feel right. Then he remembered Miss Hayden.

She was the old man’s secretary. She had worked for him for many years.

Gillian liked her but never told her. He decided to give her the one thousand dollars.

He went to her house and knocked on the door. Miss Hayden answered.

Gillian gave her the money. He said, ‘This is for you.’

He told her, ‘You were always kind to the old man.’ Miss Hayden looked surprised.

But she took the money. Gillian felt happy for the first time that day.

He went back to the lawyer’s office. He told the lawyer what he did.

The lawyer read a note from the old man. The note said if Gillian used the money in a bad way, he would get nothing.

But if he used it in a good way, he would get fifty thousand dollars. The lawyer asked Gillian to write how he used the money.

Gillian wrote, ‘I gave it to Miss Hayden. She needed it more than I did.’

The lawyer smiled. He said, ‘The old man would be proud.’

Then the lawyer told Gillian the truth. The old man left Miss Hayden only one dollar.

The rest of the money would go to Gillian. But only if he passed the test.

Gillian realized he passed the test. Now he would be rich.

He thought about how the old man wanted him to learn. Money was not just for fun or for himself.

It could help others and make life better. Gillian decided he would use his new money well.

He would find a job and work hard. He would also help people who needed it.

The next day, Gillian went to Miss Hayden’s house again. He wanted to thank her for helping him learn.

She was happy to see him. They talked for a long time about the old man.

Gillian felt he had a new start in life. He was no longer the same young man who never worked.

He had learned an important lesson. Now he had a chance to do good things.

The lawyer sent Gillian the fifty thousand dollars the next week. Gillian put some in the bank and gave some to Miss Hayden.

He also started to look for a job. He wanted to work in a company and learn new things.

Gillian knew he had much to learn. But he was ready to try.

He walked through the city again. This time, he felt different.

He saw the same people and places. But now he saw them with new eyes.

He smiled at the man selling flowers. He gave a little money to another poor man.

Gillian knew he would not forget the lesson. He would always remember what the old man taught him.

Money could be a good thing if used well. Gillian wanted to make sure he used it that way.

### B2
Gillian was a young man who had never worked a single day in his life.

His uncle, who was both rich and old, had always given him money whenever he asked.

One morning, Gillian got a letter from his uncle’s lawyer.

The letter said that his uncle had died and left him one thousand dollars in his will.

Gillian went to the lawyer’s office the next day.

The lawyer told him that the money was not just a gift but a test.

If Gillian spent it in a way that would make his uncle happy, he would receive fifty thousand dollars more.

But if he spent it badly, the money would go to someone else instead.

Gillian left the office and tried to decide what to do.

He walked through the city and looked at the people around him.

He saw a man who was selling flowers and bought a big bunch for a woman he did not know.

She smiled at him, but Gillian did not feel any joy in what he had done.

He went to a restaurant and gave some money to a poor man who was standing outside.

The man thanked him, but Gillian still felt empty inside.

He thought about buying something expensive for himself, like a car or a house.

But that did not feel right to him either.

Then he remembered his uncle’s secretary, Miss Hayden.

She was a quiet woman who had worked for his uncle for many years.

Gillian had always liked her, but he had never told her how he felt.

He decided that he would give her the one thousand dollars.

He went to her house and knocked on the door.

When she answered, he put the money in her hand.

He said, ‘This is for you, because you were always kind to my uncle.’

Miss Hayden looked surprised, but she took the money.

For the first time that day, Gillian felt happy.

He went back to the lawyer’s office and told him what he had done.

The lawyer read a note that had been written by Gillian’s uncle.

It said that if Gillian spent the money in a way that was not wise, he would get nothing more.

But if he spent it wisely, he would receive fifty thousand dollars.

The lawyer asked Gillian to write down how he had spent the money.

Gillian wrote, ‘I gave it to Miss Hayden, because she needed it more than I did.’

The lawyer smiled and said, ‘Your uncle would have been very proud of you.’

Then the lawyer told Gillian something he had not expected.

His uncle had left Miss Hayden only one dollar and the rest of his money to Gillian.

But that would only happen if Gillian had spent the one thousand dollars well.

Gillian realized that he had passed the test and would now be rich.

He felt a great sense of relief and happiness.

The lawyer explained that the test was meant to see if Gillian could think of others.

His uncle had wanted to know if Gillian would use the money for good.

Gillian understood that his uncle had given him a chance to change his life.

He decided that from that day on, he would work hard and help others.

He would not waste the money or the opportunity he had been given.

Gillian left the office with a new sense of purpose.

He knew that he would never be the same person again.

The experience had taught him the value of kindness and generosity.

He walked through the city once more, but this time he saw everything differently.

The people around him seemed happier, and the world felt full of possibilities.

He thought about Miss Hayden and how she had always been kind to his uncle.

He realized that kindness was something that should be shared with everyone.

Gillian decided that he would visit Miss Hayden again soon.

He wanted to thank her for helping him see the right way to live.

He also wanted to tell her that he would always remember what she had done.

The next few days were busy for Gillian.

He met with the lawyer several times to discuss his uncle’s will.

He learned that his uncle had left him not just money, but also property and investments.

Gillian felt a great responsibility to manage everything well.

He wanted to make sure that his uncle’s hard work would not be wasted.

He also thought about how he could use the money to help others.

He decided to start a small business that would provide jobs for people in the city.

He wanted to create something that would make his uncle proud.

Gillian knew that it would not be easy, but he was ready for the challenge.

He spent many hours planning and talking to people who could help him.

He wanted to make sure that everything was done the right way.

One evening, Gillian went to visit Miss Hayden again.

He knocked on her door and waited for her to answer.

When she opened the door, she looked surprised to see him.

Gillian smiled and said, ‘I wanted to thank you again for everything.’

Miss Hayden invited him inside and they sat down to talk.

Gillian told her about his plans for the future and how she had inspired him.

Miss Hayden listened carefully and seemed very happy for him.

She told him that his uncle would have been proud of the man he had become.

Gillian felt a deep sense of gratitude for her kindness.

He knew that he would never forget the lesson he had learned.

As he left her house, he felt a strong sense of determination.

He was ready to start his new life and make the most of the opportunity he had been given.

Gillian walked home with a smile on his face.

He knew that he was on the right path and that his future would be bright.

The test his uncle had given him had changed his life forever.

He would always remember the day he decided to give the money to Miss Hayden.

It was the day he learned the true meaning of kindness and generosity.

### C1
The morning light slanted through the half-drawn curtains of Gillian's apartment, pooling on the untouched breakfast tray that had been delivered an hour earlier-coffee grown cold, toast gone stale, the silver dome still concealing whatever dish the kitchen had sent up on a whim. He sat at the edge of the bed, fingers tracing the embossed letterhead of the envelope that had arrived with the post, its weight too slight to contain anything of consequence, yet heavy enough to press against his ribs like a stone. Inside, a single sheet of paper bore the lawyer's name in crisp, unfeeling type, and beneath it, the words that would unravel the only life he had ever known: his uncle was dead, and with him, the endless stream of funds that had sustained Gillian's aimless existence.

The lawyer's office smelled of aged leather and beeswax polish, the kind of scent that clung to places where money changed hands without ever being seen. Mr. Tolman, a man whose face seemed carved from the same wood as his desk, regarded Gillian with eyes that missed nothing-not the frayed cuff of his shirt, nor the way his fingers drummed against the armrest, betraying a restlessness he could not name. 'Your uncle left you one thousand dollars,' Tolman said, sliding a ledger across the desk, 'but not as a gift-rather, as a test.' Gillian's throat tightened; he had expected a bequest, perhaps even a reprimand, but never this-never the sense that he was being weighed, measured, found wanting before he had even spoken.

Outside, the city pulsed with its usual indifference: street vendors hawking their wares, children darting between carriages, the distant clang of a tram bell cutting through the murmur of a thousand lives lived in parallel to his own. He bought a bouquet of peonies from a girl whose hands were chapped from the cold, their petals still damp with morning dew, and pressed them into the arms of a woman who looked up in startled gratitude before hurrying away, as if kindness were a debt she could not afford to acknowledge. The gesture left him hollow, the coins in his pocket suddenly heavier than they had any right to be.

A restaurant's awning cast a sliver of shade over the pavement where a man in a threadbare coat stood, his breath visible in the autumn air. Gillian pressed a folded bill into his palm, watching as the man's eyes widened, then narrowed, as if suspecting a trick. 'God bless you,' the man muttered, but the words rang false, or perhaps it was Gillian's own guilt that made them so. He turned away before the man could thank him again, the weight of the remaining money like a brand against his skin.

Miss Hayden's boarding house stood on a quiet street where the gas lamps flickered to life earlier than in the wealthier districts, their glow pooling in the cobblestones like liquid gold. She answered the door in her usual gray dress, her hair pinned back with the same plain comb she had worn for years, and for a moment, Gillian forgot why he had come. Then he remembered the envelope in his pocket, the way it had burned against his thigh as he walked, and he held it out to her, the words spilling from him before he could stop them: 'This is for you.'

Her fingers trembled as she took the envelope, the paper crinkling under her touch like dry leaves. 'Mr. Gillian,' she began, but he cut her off, the lie coming easier than he expected: 'My uncle would have wanted you to have it-you were kind to him when no one else was.' The truth-that he had no idea what his uncle would have wanted, that he had acted on impulse, on a feeling he could not name-remained unspoken, lodged in his chest like a second heart.

Back in Tolman's office, the lawyer unfolded a second letter, its wax seal broken, its contents known only to the dead. 'If the money was spent foolishly,' Tolman read, 'the remainder of the estate passes to the state.' Gillian's pen hovered over the paper, the inkwell's surface trembling slightly from the vibration of the street below. He wrote: 'I gave it to Miss Hayden. She needed it more than I did.'

Tolman's expression did not change, but something flickered in his eyes-approval, perhaps, or pity. 'There is one more condition,' he said, sliding a third document across the desk. Gillian's breath caught as he read: his uncle had left Miss Hayden a single dollar, the rest to him-provided he had spent the thousand wisely. The room tilted, the edges of his vision blurring as the magnitude of what he had done settled over him like a shroud.

The lawyer leaned forward, his voice low. 'Your uncle wanted to know if you could be trusted with more than money-if you could be trusted with responsibility.' Gillian's hands clenched into fists, the nails biting into his palms hard enough to leave crescent moons. He had passed, but the victory felt hollow, a trick played by a man who had known him better than he knew himself.

The following days were a blur of ledgers and contracts, of meetings with bankers whose smiles did not reach their eyes and architects who spoke of foundations as if they were not, in fact, discussing the weight of a man's future. Gillian found himself standing before Miss Hayden's door again, this time with a purpose that had nothing to do with money and everything to do with the quiet way she had taken the envelope from him, her fingers brushing his for the briefest of seconds.

She listened as he spoke of his plans-a workshop for craftsmen, a fund for widows, anything to ensure his uncle's legacy did not rot in a vault. When he finished, she reached for his hand, her grip firmer than he expected. 'Your uncle would have been proud,' she said, and for the first time in years, Gillian believed it was true.

As he walked home, the city no longer felt like a maze of strangers but a place where every face held a story, every outstretched hand a chance to do better than he had the day before. The thousand dollars had been a test, but the fifty thousand were a beginning, and Gillian intended to make them count.

## st-0217 — The Third Ingredient
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
Hetty lives in a small house. The house is in a big city.

She works hard every day. She pays for her room. She has little money after.

Hetty often feels no food. She can not buy much food.

One night, Hetty comes home. She feels very bad.

She has only a little money. She has no food in her house.

She thinks of her mother’s food. She can not buy food.

Hetty sits at her table. She does not know what to do.

Then she hears a sound at the door. It is Cecilia from next door.

Cecilia holds a small thing. She looks kind at Hetty.

She says, 'I make too much food.' 'You can have some.'

Hetty says thank you. She takes the thing.

The food looks good. But it is not enough for two times.

Hetty puts water in the food. She moves it with a big thing.

She hears another sound at the door. It is Joe from above.

Joe looks not sure. He holds a small thing.

He says, 'I find this.' 'I do not know what it is.'

Hetty takes the thing. She says thank you to Joe.

She puts the thing in the food. She lets it sit there.

Hetty thinks about the people next door. They both come to her door.

Then she hears a sound again. It is Mrs. Flannigan.

Mrs. Flannigan holds some food. She says, 'I buy too much.'

Hetty takes the food. She puts it in the thing.

Soon the house feels great. Hetty has good food now.

She feels good and calls her friends. They all come to eat.

They sit together at the table. They talk and eat the food.

The food is very good. Everyone likes it.

Hetty feels full and good. She feels good because of her friends.

After that night they share food. They help each other.

Hetty knows now. Kind things can make good happen.

### A2
There was a small house in a big city. The house was on a street.

Hetty lived there. She worked hard every day.

She had little money after she paid for her room. Hetty often felt she needed food.

She could not buy much food. One evening, Hetty came home.

She felt very low on power. She had only a few small coins.

She had nothing in her place for food. Hetty thought about her mother’s food.

Her mother used to make it long ago. But Hetty had no money for food parts.

She sat at her table. She did not know what to do.

Then she heard a sound at the door. It was Cecilia, who lived near her.

Cecilia was a kind woman. She lived by herself.

Cecilia held a small thing for food. She smiled and said, ‘Hetty, I made too much.’

‘I thought you might want some.’ Hetty said thank you and took it inside.

The food smelled nice. But Hetty knew it was not enough for two times.

She decided to put water in it. She wanted to make it last more time.

As she mixed, she heard another sound. This time, it was Joe.

Joe lived in the room over hers. He looked not sure and held a small thing.

He said, ‘I found this in my thing for cold days.’ ‘I do not know how it got there.’

Hetty took it and said thank you. She did not know why he gave it to her.

Hetty put it in the food. She let it cook for a while.

She thought it was not usual. Both her near people came to her door that night.

Just as she was going to eat, there was a sound again. It was Mrs. Flannigan.

Mrs. Flannigan was an old woman. She lived down the way in the house.

She held a small part of food. She said, ‘I bought too much today.’

‘I can not eat it all.’ Hetty took it and put it in the thing for food.

Soon, the room smelled very nice. Hetty saw she had all parts of real food.

She laughed and felt she had good luck. Hetty asked her near people to share the food.

They all sat together. They talked while they ate.

The food was very good. Everyone said it was the best in a long time.

Hetty felt happy and full. She was happy not just from the food.

She was happy from her friends’ kind act. After that night, Hetty and her near people often shared food.

### B1
In a big city, there was a small house on a quiet street. A young woman named Hetty lived there.

Hetty worked hard every day. She did not have much money left after she paid for her room. She often felt she needed more food because she could not buy enough.

One evening, Hetty came home tired. She had only a little money in her pocket. There was nothing in her small food place. She thought about the food her mother used to make. But she had no money to buy what she needed.

Hetty sat at her table. She did not know what to do. Then she heard a knock at the door.

It was her neighbor, Cecilia. Cecilia was a kind woman who lived alone. She held a small dish and smiled. She said, 'Hetty, I made too much food today.' 'I thought you might want some.'

Hetty thanked her and took the dish inside. The food smelled good. But she knew it was not enough for two meals. She decided to add water to make it last longer. As she mixed it, she heard another knock.

This time, it was Joe. Joe lived in the room above Hetty. He looked a bit quiet and held out a small bag. He said, 'I found this in my coat.' 'I do not know how it got there.'

Hetty took the bag and thanked him. She wondered why he gave it to her. She added what was inside to the food and let it cook.

Hetty thought it was strange. Both her neighbors had come to her door that evening. Just as she was about to eat, there was another knock.

It was Mrs. Flannigan, an old woman from down the hall. She held a small piece of meat. She said, 'I bought too much today.' 'I will not eat it all.'

Hetty took the meat and added it to the dish. Soon, the kitchen smelled very good. Hetty saw she had all the parts of a real meal. She laughed and thought she was lucky.

Hetty asked her neighbors to share the food. They all sat together and talked while they ate. The food was very good. Everyone said it was the best they had eaten in a long time.

Hetty felt happy and full. She was happy not just because of the food. She was happy because her friends were kind.

After that night, Hetty and her neighbors often ate together. They saw that if they helped each other, they would have enough. Hetty always remembered that evening. A simple thing from each person made a big meal.

The meal brought people together. Sometimes, the best things come from places you do not expect. If you share a little, you may get more than you give. Hetty knew this was true.

One day, Cecilia said, 'If we all bring something small, we can eat well.' Joe agreed and said, 'I will bring what I can.' Mrs. Flannigan smiled and said, 'I will too.' Hetty was glad they had started this new way.

Now, they meet often in the evening. They talk and laugh while they eat. Hetty feels she has more than before. She has good food and good friends.

The neighbors have learned something important. When people work together, life is better. Hetty is happy she knocked on their doors that first night. She knows they will continue to share.

Hetty still thinks about that evening sometimes. She has never forgotten how it felt to be so hungry. But now, she knows she is not alone. Her neighbors will always help if she needs it.

One morning, Hetty woke up early. She decided to make breakfast for everyone. She knocked on each door and invited them to come. Soon, the kitchen was full of laughter and good smells.

They all sat down to eat together. Hetty smiled as she watched her friends. She realized that sharing made everything better. It was not just about the food, but about the company too.

From that day on, they made a plan. Each person would bring something different each week. This way, they would always have enough to eat. They would never feel hungry again.

Hetty felt proud of what they had started. She knew it was a small thing, but it made a big difference. Her life had changed because of her neighbors. She was grateful for their kindness every day.

### B2
In a big city, there was a small house on a quiet street. Inside lived a young woman named Hetty.

She worked hard every day. But she had little money left after she paid for her room.

Often, Hetty did not have enough to eat. She felt empty inside because she could buy only a little food.

One evening, Hetty came home tired. She had only a few coins in her pocket and no food at home.

She thought about the meal her mother used to make. But she had no money to buy meat or vegetables.

Hetty sat at her table and let out a long breath. She did not know what she would do.

Just then, she heard a knock at the door. It was her neighbor, Cecilia, a woman who lived alone.

Cecilia held a small pot and smiled. She said, 'Hetty, I made too much food today.'

She thought you might like some,' Cecilia added. Hetty thanked her and took the pot inside.

The food smelled nice, but Hetty knew it was not enough for two days. She decided to add water to make it last.

As she stirred, she heard another knock. This time, it was a young man named Joe, who lived above her.

Joe looked unsure and held out a small bag. He said, 'I found this in my coat pocket.'

I do not know how it got there,' he continued. Hetty took it and thanked him.

She did not understand why he gave it to her. But she put it in the pot with the food.

Hetty thought it was strange that both her neighbors had come to her door. Just as she was about to eat, there was another knock.

It was Mrs. Flannigan, an elderly woman from down the hall. She held a small piece of meat.

Mrs. Flannigan said, 'I bought too much today.' 'I will not eat it all,' she added.

Hetty took the meat and added it to the pot. Soon, the kitchen smelled very good.

Hetty saw that she now had all the parts of a real meal. She laughed and felt lucky.

Hetty decided to ask her neighbors to join her. She wanted to share what she had.

They all sat together and talked while they ate. The food was very good, and everyone said it was the best they had tasted in a long time.

Hetty felt happy and full. She was happy not just because of the food, but because of her friends.

After that night, Hetty and her neighbors often shared meals. They saw that by helping each other, they could all have enough.

Hetty never forgot how a simple vegetable, a bit of meat, and some food from a neighbor could bring people together. Sometimes, the best things in life come from places you do not expect.

One day, Hetty sat with Cecilia and talked about that evening. She said, 'If you had not brought me that food, I would not have had enough to eat.'

Cecilia smiled and replied, 'If Joe had not found that vegetable, the meal would not have been as good.' They both laughed.

Hetty thought about how small acts could change everything. She realized that kindness was more important than money.

The next week, Hetty saw Joe carrying a heavy bag. She asked if he needed help.

Joe said, 'I would be grateful if you could hold this for a moment.' Hetty helped him, and they talked while he sorted his things.

Joe told her, 'The food we shared that night was the best I have had in a long time.' Hetty smiled and said she felt the same.

From then on, Hetty made sure to check on her neighbors often. She wanted to return the kindness they had shown her.

One evening, Hetty was cooking when she heard a knock. It was Mrs. Flannigan, who looked worried.

Mrs. Flannigan said, 'I do not feel well today.' 'I was wondering if you could help me.'

Hetty invited her in and made her some tea. She sat with her until Mrs. Flannigan felt better.

Mrs. Flannigan thanked her and said, 'You are a good friend.' Hetty felt proud that she could help.

Later, Hetty thought about how her life had changed. She had gone from feeling alone to having friends who cared about her.

She remembered the first night when she had nothing. Now, she had people she could rely on.

Hetty decided to make a big meal for everyone. She wanted to show her thanks.

She bought vegetables and meat with the little money she had. She invited Cecilia, Joe, and Mrs. Flannigan to join her.

As they ate, they talked about their lives. Hetty felt happy to be part of such a good group.

Joe said, 'If we all share what we have, no one will go without.' Everyone agreed.

Hetty realized that this was the true meaning of community. It was not about how much you had, but about how much you gave.

The next month, Hetty started a small group. They met once a week to share food and talk.

More people from the building joined. Soon, it was not just Hetty and her neighbors, but many others too.

One day, a new woman moved into the building. Her name was Clara, and she looked very tired.

Hetty saw her and thought about how she had felt when she first moved in. She decided to invite Clara to the next meal.

Clara came and was surprised to see so many people. She said, 'I did not expect such kindness.'

Hetty smiled and said, 'This is what we do here.' Clara felt welcome and happy.

Over time, the group grew even larger. People who had never talked before became friends.

Hetty was glad she had started it. She knew it made a big difference in everyone's life.

One evening, as they all sat together, Hetty looked around. She saw smiles on every face.

She thought about how far they had all come. It all started with a simple pot of food and a vegetable.

Hetty knew that no matter what happened, they would always help each other. That was the most important thing.

### C1
The house stood like a quiet sentinel at the end of a street that had long since forgotten the clatter of children or the bark of dogs. Inside, Hetty sat at the scarred wooden table, her fingers tracing the grain as if it might yield answers to the hollow ache in her stomach. The gas lamp sputtered, casting long shadows that stretched toward the empty cupboard, a silent testament to the day's meager earnings. She had worked twelve hours at the laundry, her hands raw from the lye, her back stiff from bending over steaming tubs, yet the coins in her pocket felt lighter than air. Outside, the city hummed with life-laughter from a distant pub, the clatter of hooves on cobblestones-but inside, the silence was thick enough to taste.

Hetty exhaled, a slow, measured breath that did nothing to ease the gnawing emptiness. She thought of her mother's kitchen, the way the scent of thyme and rosemary would curl through the air, rich and warm, like an embrace. Now, the only thing curling through her own kitchen was the damp chill of evening, seeping through the cracks in the window frame. She had meant to buy potatoes, perhaps a scrap of bacon, but the coins had slipped through her fingers like sand, spent on rent, on soap, on the barest necessities. A sigh escaped her, soft and defeated, as she rested her forehead against the cool surface of the table.

A knock at the door startled her upright, her heart leaping before she could remind herself that no one ever came calling with good news. She smoothed her apron, its once-bright pattern now faded to a ghost of itself, and pulled the door open just enough to peer into the dim hallway. Cecilia stood there, her round face framed by a kerchief, a small enamel pot cradled in her hands like an offering. "I made too much," she said, her voice warm with the kind of generosity that didn't ask for thanks, only acceptance. Hetty hesitated-pride and hunger warred briefly-before stepping aside to let her in.

The stew inside the pot was thin, little more than broth with a few carrots bobbing like lost ships in a vast sea, but the scent of it filled the room, rich and savory. Hetty's mouth watered as she stirred, the wooden spoon clinking against the sides, each turn releasing another wave of warmth. She knew it wouldn't last-one meal, perhaps two if she stretched it with water-but for now, it was enough to quiet the growling in her stomach. As she set the pot to simmer, another knock echoed through the room, sharper this time, as if the visitor were unsure of their welcome. Joe stood in the doorway, his lanky frame hunched slightly, a small burlap bag dangling from his fingers like a question.

"Found this in my coat pocket," he muttered, his voice rough with embarrassment, as if the turnip inside were a secret he'd been caught keeping. Hetty took the bag, her fingers brushing against the rough fabric, and peered inside at the gnarled root, its skin mottled with earth. She didn't ask how it had ended up in his pocket, or why he'd chosen to give it to her-some things didn't need explaining. Instead, she simply nodded, her throat tight, and added it to the pot, where it sank slowly, as if reluctant to join the meager feast. The broth darkened slightly, the turnip's earthy sweetness mingling with the carrots, and for the first time that evening, Hetty allowed herself a flicker of hope.

The third knock came just as she was lifting the spoon to her lips, the steam curling around her face like a promise. Mrs. Flannigan stood in the hallway, her silver hair escaping its pins, a small parcel wrapped in brown paper clutched in her hands. "I bought too much meat," she said, her voice brisk but her eyes soft, as if she already knew the weight of what she was offering. Hetty took the package, the paper cool against her skin, and unwrapped it to reveal a piece of salted pork, its edges glistening with fat. Without a word, she added it to the pot, and the room filled with the sizzle of fat hitting the broth, the scent so rich it made her dizzy.

The meal that emerged was no grand feast, but it was whole-vegetables, meat, broth, each ingredient finding its place in the pot as if they had been waiting for one another all along. Hetty laughed then, a sound so unexpected it startled even her, and the weight in her chest lifted just a little. She set the table with her chipped plates, the mismatched cutlery, and called her neighbors in, their faces alight with curiosity and something like anticipation. They sat together, the four of them, their chairs scraping against the floor, and for a moment, there was only the clink of spoons and the quiet hum of contentment. The stew was simple, but it was more than enough-enough to fill their stomachs, enough to fill the silence, enough to remind them that they were not alone.

After that night, the pot never stayed empty for long. Hetty would set it on the stove each evening, and one by one, her neighbors would appear, each with something to add-a handful of barley, a sprig of herbs, a scrap of cheese. They learned the rhythm of one another's lives, the way Cecilia's laughter could fill a room, the way Joe's quiet observations made them all pause and think. Mrs. Flannigan, who had once been a stranger, now sat in Hetty's kitchen as if she had always belonged, her stories weaving through the steam like threads in a tapestry. Hetty no longer counted the coins in her pocket or measured the emptiness of her cupboards-she measured the warmth of the room, the way it hummed with life.

One evening, as the group gathered around the table, Hetty looked at each of them in turn-Cecilia's flour-dusted hands, Joe's ink-stained fingers, Mrs. Flannigan's trembling grip on her spoon-and felt a surge of gratitude so strong it nearly stole her breath. She had come to the city with nothing, not even the certainty that she would find a place for herself, but here, in this small kitchen, she had found something far greater. Joe, who had once been little more than a shadow in the hallway, now leaned forward and said, "If we all share what we have, no one will go without." Hetty smiled, her heart full, and knew he was right-not just about the food, but about everything. The next week, she invited Clara, the new woman from the third floor, her face drawn with exhaustion, her eyes wary as a stray cat's.

Clara hesitated in the doorway, her fingers twisting the hem of her shawl, but the scent of the stew drew her in, and soon she was sitting at the table, her shoulders relaxing inch by inch. "I didn't expect this," she admitted, her voice barely above a whisper, as if kindness were a language she was still learning. Hetty passed her a bowl, the steam rising between them like a bridge, and said, "This is what we do here." Clara's eyes filled, but she didn't look away-she met Hetty's gaze and nodded, as if a silent agreement had been struck. By the end of the evening, Clara was laughing, her voice mingling with the others, and Hetty knew she had found her place among them.

The group grew, as groups do when they are built on something true. People who had once passed one another in the hallway without a word now stopped to talk, to share, to ask after one another's days. Hetty watched it all with quiet satisfaction, her kitchen the heart of something larger than herself, something that pulsed with life and warmth. One evening, as they sat around the table, the room alive with chatter and the clatter of dishes, she looked around and realized that this-this was what she had been searching for all along. Not wealth, not security, but this: the simple, unshakable knowledge that she was part of something, that she belonged.

Years later, when Hetty looked back on that first night, she would smile at how small it had all begun-a pot of broth, a turnip, a scrap of meat. But small things have a way of growing, of taking root in the most unexpected places, and before long, they become something unshakable. She had come to the city alone, but she had left it with a family, one built not on blood, but on kindness, on the quiet understanding that we are all, in the end, just looking for a place to belong. And as long as there was a pot on the stove and a table to gather around, she knew they would always find it.

## st-0218 — A Scandal in Bohemia
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
A man lives in a big city in 1888. His name is Sherlock Holmes. He has a good job. He helps people with hard problems.

One night, a man comes to his house. The man does not say his real name. He calls himself Count Kramm. But Holmes knows it is not true.

The man takes off his black coat. Holmes sees he is a leader. The leader is from a far country. He has a big problem.

The leader will have an important day soon. A woman has a photo of them. Her name is Irene Adler. She wants money from the leader.

She will send the photo if he does not pay. She will do this soon. The leader does not want this. He asks Holmes to get the photo back.

The important day is in three days. Holmes puts on old clothes. He looks like a worker. He goes to where Irene lives.

He wants to learn about her life. He meets some of her friends. They say she is kind. The next day, Holmes puts on other clothes.

He waits near her house. He sees a man run out. The man calls a car. He asks Irene to come with him.

They go to a big house. Holmes follows them. He looks like he works there. After, Irene and the man go home.

Holmes goes back to his house. Holmes makes a plan with his friend. His friend’s name is Watson. They go to Irene’s house the next night.

Holmes knows where she keeps the photo. He tells Watson what to do. Watson puts a small thing through the door. He calls 'fire!'

Irene runs to keep the photo. Holmes sees where she puts it. But then she looks at Holmes. She says his name.

No one ever does this to him. The next morning, Holmes and the leader go. They want to take the photo. But the house is not full.

Irene and her man are gone. She leaves a letter for Holmes. She says she will not use the photo. She keeps it to stay well.

The leader thanks Holmes. But Holmes says Irene is very good. He keeps her photo in his house. He always remembers her.

The case is over.

### A2
In eighteen eighty-eight, a man lived in London. His name was Sherlock Holmes.

He was very good at finding answers. Many people came to his house for help.

One evening, a man arrived. He did not say his name.

He called himself Count Kramm. But Holmes knew this was not true.

The man took off his outer clothes. Holmes saw he was really a leader from a far country.

The leader had a big problem. He was going to marry a woman soon.

She was from another family with power. But a woman called Irene Adler had a picture.

The picture showed the leader and her together. She wanted to send the picture to the leader’s future wife’s family.

She asked for money. The leader felt worry.

The picture could cause trouble. It could also cause trouble for his country.

He asked Holmes to get the picture back. The marriage was in three days.

Holmes said yes. He put on old clothes to look like a poor worker.

He went to the street where Irene Adler lived. He wanted to learn about her life.

He met some of her friends. They said she was kind.

The next day, Holmes wore different clothes. He looked like a man who worked with animals.

He waited near her house. He saw a man run out.

The man’s name was Godfrey Norton. He called a car to take him away.

He asked Irene to come with him to a church. They had to go fast.

Holmes followed them. He acted like he was there for the wedding.

After the wedding, Irene and Norton went home. Holmes went back to his house.

Holmes made a plan with his friend Watson. They wanted to get the picture.

They went to Irene’s house the next evening. She was not at home.

Holmes knew she kept the picture in her sitting room. He asked Watson to throw something through the window.

Watson threw it. He called out, 'Fire!'

Irene ran to save her picture. Holmes saw where she took it from.

She pulled the picture out from the wall. Then she looked at Holmes and smiled.

She said, 'I know who you are, Mr. Holmes.' Holmes felt surprise.

No one had ever done this to him before. He did not know what to do.

The next morning, Holmes and the leader went to Irene’s house. They wanted to take the picture.

But the house was empty. Irene and her new husband had left the country.

She left a letter for Holmes. She said she would not cause trouble for the leader.

She kept the picture only to keep herself from harm. The leader felt happy.

He thanked Holmes for his help. But Holmes said Irene Adler was the only woman to do this.

He kept a picture of her in his house. He always spoke of her with respect.

The case was over. But Holmes never forgot the woman from Bohemia.

### B1
In the year eighteen eighty-eight, a man named Sherlock Holmes lived in London. He was very good at solving hard problems. Many people came to his house to ask for help.

One evening, a man arrived at Holmes’s door. He did not say his real name. He said his name was Count Kramm. But Holmes knew this was not true.

The man took off his coat and hat. Holmes saw he was really a king. The king came from a country far away. He had a big problem.

The king would marry a woman soon. But a lady named Irene Adler had a photograph. The photograph showed the king and Irene together. She wanted money or she would send the photograph.

The king was afraid. The photograph could cause trouble for his marriage. He asked Holmes to get the photograph back. The wedding was in three days.

Holmes agreed to help. He put on old clothes. He looked like a poor worker. He went to Irene Adler’s street to learn about her.

Holmes met some of her friends. They said she was kind and smart. The next day, Holmes dressed as a man who worked with horses. He waited near her house.

Holmes saw a man named Godfrey Norton run out. Norton called a coach. He asked Irene to come with him to a church. Holmes followed them.

Holmes acted like he would help at their wedding. After the wedding, Irene and Norton went home. Holmes returned to his own house.

Holmes made a plan with his friend Watson. They would go to Irene’s house the next evening. Holmes knew she kept the photograph in her sitting room. He asked Watson to throw something that made smoke and shout 'fire.'

When Irene heard 'fire,' she ran to save her photograph. Holmes would see where she hid it. At first, everything went as they planned. Irene pulled the photograph from a part of the wall.

But then she looked at Holmes and smiled. She said, 'I know who you are, Mr. Holmes.' Holmes was very surprised. No one had ever done this to him before.

The next morning, Holmes and the king went to Irene’s house. They wanted to take the photograph. But the house was empty. Irene and her new husband had left the country.

Irene left a letter for Holmes. She said she would not cause trouble for the king. She kept the photograph to keep herself safe. The king was happy and thanked Holmes.

But Holmes said Irene Adler was the only woman who did better than him. He kept a photograph of her in his house. He always spoke of her with respect. The case was over, but Holmes never forgot her.

If anyone asks Holmes about this case, he will tell the story again. He has never met a woman like Irene since then. She made a big mark on his life. He thinks she was the smartest person he ever knew.

Holmes often remembers how she solved the problem. He has kept her photograph for many years. If he sees it, he will think of her again. She was different from other people he met.

The king returned to his country without trouble. He has told his family about Holmes’s help. If he needs help again, he will come back. But he hopes he will not need it.

Holmes still works on many cases. He has solved many hard problems since then. But he will always remember Irene Adler. She was the one who surprised him the most.

Irene Adler was not like most people. She was quick to understand things. She could see what others could not. Holmes knew she was special from the start.

### B2
In the year eighteen eighty-eight, a man called Sherlock Holmes lived in London.

He was very good at solving problems that others could not solve.

Many people came to his house to ask for help with strange cases.

One evening, a man arrived who did not give his real name.

He called himself the Count of Kramm, but Holmes knew this was not true.

The man took off his coat and hat.

Holmes saw he was truly a king.

The king came from a country far away and had a big problem.

He was going to marry a woman from another royal family soon.

But a woman named Irene Adler had a picture of the two of them together.

She wanted to send the picture to the king’s future wife’s family.

She would do this if he did not pay her money.

The king feared the picture would ruin his marriage.

He also feared it would harm peace in his country.

He asked Holmes to get the picture back before the wedding in three days.

Holmes agreed and put on old clothes to look like a poor worker.

He went to the street where Irene Adler lived to learn about her.

There, he met some of her friends.

They told him she was a kind and clever woman.

The next day, Holmes dressed as a worker who looked after horses.

He waited near her house.

He saw a man named Godfrey Norton run out and call for a ride.

Norton asked Irene to come with him to a church quickly.

Holmes followed them and pretended to be a witness at their wedding.

After the wedding, Irene and Norton went home.

Holmes returned to his own house.

Holmes made a plan with his friend Watson to get the picture.

They went to Irene’s house the next evening when she was out.

Holmes knew she kept the picture in her sitting room.

He asked Watson to throw a small thing that made smoke through the window.

Watson was to shout 'fire.'

When Irene ran to save her picture, Holmes would see where she hid it.

Everything went as Holmes planned at first.

Irene ran to a part of the wall and pulled out the picture.

But then she looked at Holmes and smiled.

She said, 'I know who you are, Mr. Holmes.'

Holmes was very surprised because no one had ever done better than him before.

The next morning, Holmes and the king went to Irene’s house.

They wanted to take the picture, but the house was empty.

Irene and her new husband had left the country during the night.

She left a letter for Holmes.

In it, she said she would not cause trouble for the king.

She kept the picture only to protect herself from him in the future.

The king was happy and thanked Holmes for his help.

But Holmes said Irene Adler was the only woman who had ever done better than him.

He kept a picture of her in his house to remember her.

From that day on, he always spoke of her with great respect.

The case was over, but Holmes never forgot the clever woman from Bohemia.

He often thought about how she had solved the problem in her own way.

She had shown that even the best solver of problems could be surprised.

The king returned to his country and prepared for his wedding.

He felt safe because Irene would not use the picture against him.

Holmes and Watson talked about the case many times.

They agreed that Irene Adler was one of the most interesting people they had met.

Holmes said that if he had met her under different conditions, he might have liked her even more.

He also said that she had a rare ability to understand people quickly.

Watson wrote about the case in his notes.

He described how Holmes had been both impressed and disappointed by what happened.

The case became known among those who knew Holmes well.

It showed that even the best solver of problems could face a challenge he did not expect.

Irene Adler’s name was mentioned often in their talks.

Holmes would sometimes smile when he spoke of her.

He never forgot how she had used his own plan against him.

It was a lesson he remembered in all his future cases.

The king’s wedding went ahead as planned, and peace was kept in his country.

No one ever saw the picture again, and the matter was closed.

But for Holmes, the memory of Irene Adler stayed with him.

She was a woman who had done what no one else could do.

She had done better than the great Sherlock Holmes at his own game.

That was something he would always remember.

Later, Holmes thought about the case again.

He wondered what would have happened if Irene had not been so clever.

Perhaps the king would have lost his chance to marry the royal woman.

The country might have faced trouble without the marriage.

Holmes felt that Irene had acted in a way that was both smart and fair.

She had not used the picture to hurt the king, only to protect herself.

This made Holmes respect her even more.

Watson noticed that Holmes often spoke of her with admiration.

He said it was rare for Holmes to speak so highly of anyone.

Irene Adler had truly made a strong impression on him.

The two friends sometimes sat in silence, thinking about the case.

They both knew it was one they would never forget.

The story of Irene Adler became a favorite topic when they talked.

Holmes would say that she was the only person who had ever surprised him so much.

He admitted that he had learned a lot from her.

She had shown him that there was always more to learn.

The case had changed the way he looked at problems.

He began to think more about the people involved, not just the facts.

This helped him solve many other cases in the years that followed.

Irene Adler remained a special memory for both Holmes and Watson.

She had done what no one else had done before.

She had made the great Sherlock Holmes think in new ways.

And for that, he would always be grateful.

### C1
London, in the waning days of eighteen eighty-eight, lay draped in a pall of coal-smoke and fog, its gas lamps flickering like restless spirits along Baker Street. Within the dimly lit confines of 221B, Sherlock Holmes stood motionless by the window, his sharp profile etched against the amber glow of the fire, fingers steepled beneath his chin as if weighing the very air for secrets. The evening post had brought nothing of interest, and the silence was broken only by the occasional crackle of the hearth and the distant clatter of hooves on cobblestones-until, that is, a visitor arrived who would leave an indelible mark on the detective's reputation.

The knock at the door was neither timid nor bold, but measured, as though the caller sought to convey urgency without desperation. Mrs. Hudson admitted a man whose bearing suggested nobility, though his features were obscured by a heavy travelling cloak and a hat pulled low over his brow. He introduced himself as the Count von Kramm, a name Holmes dismissed with a faint, knowing smile before the man had even removed his gloves. As the stranger shed his outer garments, the lamplight revealed not the countenance of a minor European aristocrat, but the unmistakable, weary dignity of a monarch-one whose kingdom lay far from England's shores.

The king-for such he was-spoke in a voice laced with the precise cadence of one accustomed to command, though his hands betrayed a tremor as he clasped them together. His dilemma was simple in its outline, yet fraught with consequence: a photograph, taken in a moment of youthful indiscretion, now threatened to unravel the delicate threads of a royal marriage alliance. Irene Adler, the woman who possessed the image, was no common blackmailer; she was an opera singer of rare talent, a woman whose intelligence and independence had already earned her a reputation in the salons of Europe. If the photograph were to reach the family of his intended bride, the scandal would not only dissolve the engagement but risk plunging his country into political turmoil.

Holmes listened in silence, his gaze never leaving the king's face, absorbing not just the words but the unspoken fears beneath them. When the king concluded, the detective rose with the fluid grace of a predator scenting prey and declared, 'The photograph must be recovered before the wedding-three days hence.' His plan began with observation, and so, the following morning, Holmes emerged from his lodgings not as the consulting detective, but as a grizzled stable-hand, his face smudged with soot, his clothes reeking of hay and horseflesh. The streets of St. John's Wood, where Irene Adler resided, were quiet at that hour, but Holmes moved with purpose, his sharp eyes missing nothing-not the milkman's whistle, nor the way the curtains twitched in the house across from hers.

By midday, he had struck up a conversation with a group of workmen loitering near a public house, their laughter coarse but their tongues loose with gossip. From them, he gleaned fragments of Irene Adler's character: her generosity to the poor, her sharp wit, the way she carried herself as though she answered to no one. Yet it was not until the afternoon that Holmes witnessed something far more intriguing-a man, later identified as Godfrey Norton, bursting from her door in evident agitation, hailing a hansom with a frantic wave. Norton's urgency was matched only by Irene's swift appearance at the threshold, her expression unreadable as she stepped into the cab beside him, the two of them speeding off toward the church of St. Monica in Edgware Road.

Holmes, ever resourceful, followed on foot, arriving at the church just as the ceremony was concluding. Slipping inside, he positioned himself near the altar, his rough attire blending seamlessly with the handful of witnesses who had gathered on short notice. When the priest asked if anyone present knew of any impediment to the marriage, Holmes stepped forward, his voice gruff but clear, and declared none. The deed done, the newlyweds departed, leaving Holmes to ponder the implications: Irene Adler was no longer a woman alone, but one with a husband who might prove either an ally or an obstacle in the days to come.

That evening, back in Baker Street, Holmes outlined his strategy to Watson, his words clipped with the precision of a man who had already seen the end of the game. 'Tomorrow, we shall give Miss Adler-or rather, Mrs. Norton-cause to reveal the photograph's hiding place,' he said, holding up a small, glass vial filled with a pungent, yellowish liquid. Watson, ever the skeptic, frowned. 'You intend to frighten her?' 'I intend to *motivate* her,' Holmes corrected, his eyes gleaming. 'Fear is a powerful catalyst, and a woman of her intelligence will not be fooled for long.'

The following night, under a sky thick with clouds, Holmes and Watson approached Irene Adler's house, its windows glowing warmly against the chill air. Holmes had already ascertained that the photograph was concealed somewhere in the sitting room, and so, with Watson stationed at the ready, he rapped sharply on the front door. When no answer came, he nodded to his companion, who hurled the vial through an open window, its contents erupting into a thick, acrid smoke that billowed into the room. A moment later, Watson's voice rang out, clear and urgent: 'Fire! Fire in the house!'

The response was immediate-Irene Adler burst from the front door, her face pale but composed, only to freeze upon seeing Holmes standing in the shadows, his expression unreadable. Without hesitation, she turned and dashed back inside, her skirts swirling as she made straight for a panel in the wall, which she pried open with practiced ease. There, in the hidden compartment, lay the photograph, its edges slightly curled with age. As she clutched it to her chest, she turned to Holmes, her lips curving into a smile that held no malice, only a quiet, triumphant amusement.

'I must congratulate you, Mr. Holmes,' she said, her voice steady. 'Few men have ever outwitted me, and none have done so with such elegance.' Holmes, for once, was at a loss for words, his usual composure giving way to something akin to admiration. Before he could respond, she inclined her head slightly, as though acknowledging a worthy opponent, and disappeared into the house, leaving him standing in the dim light, the weight of her words settling over him like a challenge.

The next morning, Holmes and the king arrived at Irene Adler's residence, only to find the house silent and the door unlocked. Inside, the air was thick with the scent of lavender and beeswax, but the rooms were empty, save for a single envelope addressed to Holmes in a hand he recognized all too well. The letter within was brief but unequivocal: Irene Adler would not use the photograph against the king, nor would she ever trouble him again. She had kept it only as insurance, a safeguard against the whims of a man who had once loved her and might, in the future, seek to harm her.

The king exhaled, his relief palpable, though Holmes remained silent, his fingers tracing the edge of the photograph she had left behind-a memento, perhaps, or a reminder of the one case that had bested him. As they departed, the detective's mind was already turning over the events of the past three days, dissecting each moment, each decision, with the precision of a surgeon. Irene Adler had not merely outmaneuvered him; she had done so with a grace and intelligence that left him both chastened and intrigued. In the years that followed, her name would surface in his conversations with Watson, not as a failure, but as a rare and fascinating exception-proof that even the most astute mind could be surprised.

---
