### B1
In the south of England, there is a small town called King’s Pyland. People in the town love animals very much.

The best animal is a horse named Silver Blaze. He runs very fast and is very strong. His coat is bright and looks good. His owner is Colonel Ross.

Colonel Ross hopes Silver Blaze will win the big race soon. One evening, a week before the race, Colonel Ross goes to the stable. He wants to check on Silver Blaze. The stable is quiet, and the door is locked.

But something does not feel right. The stable boy, Hunter, is inside. He does not answer when the Colonel calls. The Colonel opens the door and finds Hunter on the floor.

Hunter is hurt and does not move. Colonel Ross sends for the police right away. The police look for Silver Blaze everywhere. They cannot find him anywhere.

The stable is empty. No one in the town has seen the horse that night. Colonel Ross is very worried. He decides to ask a well-known man for help.

The next morning, Sherlock Holmes and Dr. Watson arrive at King’s Pyland. Holmes looks at the stable and the ground near it. He sees strange marks by the door. He finds a few red drops on the ground.

He also sees a small piece of cloth on a small plant. Holmes asks many questions about the people who work with Silver Blaze. A man named Fitzroy Simpson visited the stable the night before. Simpson asked many questions about Silver Blaze.

He even offered Hunter money to tell him things. Hunter said no, but later he felt very tired. Holmes also talks to Silas Brown. Brown trains horses at a farm near King’s Pyland.

Brown is not easy to talk to. He had argued with Colonel Ross before. Holmes sees that Brown’s shoes have the same strange marks as near the stable. Brown tries to explain, but Holmes does not believe him.

The next day, Holmes and Watson walk near the farm. They see a group of horses in a field. One horse looks like Silver Blaze, but it is not the same color. Holmes smiles and says, 'I think we have found him, Watson.'

Someone has put paint on the horse to change his look. But Holmes knows it is Silver Blaze. Holmes explains that Silas Brown took the horse. Brown wanted to stop Silver Blaze from running in the race.

Colonel Ross is very happy. Silver Blaze is safe just in time for the race. On the day of the big race, Silver Blaze runs faster than ever. He wins the race easily.

Everyone is happy for the strong horse. Holmes and Watson leave King’s Pyland. They are happy they solved the problem. As they walk away, Holmes says, 'The most important thing was what did not happen.'

Watson asks what he means, but Holmes does not explain. The next day, Watson writes about the case. He thinks about the strange marks and the red drops. He remembers how Holmes found the piece of cloth.

Watson knows Holmes is very good at solving problems. He hopes to learn more from him in the future. The case of Silver Blaze shows how smart Holmes is. It also shows how much people in the town care about the horse.

The race was very important to everyone. Now, the town can celebrate the victory together. People will remember this day for a long time. They are glad Silver Blaze is safe and back home.

### B2
In the south of England, there was a small town called King’s Pyland.

People in the village loved animals. They loved horses most of all.

The best horse in the village was named Silver Blaze.

Silver Blaze had a bright silver coat. He was very fast and strong.

His owner, Colonel Ross, hoped Silver Blaze would win the big race soon.

The race was important. Many people wanted to see who would win.

One evening, seven days before the race, Colonel Ross went to the stable.

He wanted to check on Silver Blaze before the night was over.

The stable was quiet. The door was locked from the outside.

But the Colonel felt something was wrong.

He called the stable boy, Hunter, but there was no answer.

When the Colonel opened the door, he saw Hunter on the floor.

Hunter was not moving and looked hurt.

Colonel Ross quickly sent for the police to help.

The police looked inside the stable but could not find Silver Blaze.

The horse was gone. There were no signs of him anywhere.

The police searched the village but found nothing.

Nobody had seen Silver Blaze that night.

Colonel Ross was very worried. He did not know what to do.

He decided to ask for help from a man who solved problems well.

The next morning, Sherlock Holmes and his friend Dr. Watson arrived.

Holmes looked at the stable and the ground near it carefully.

He saw some marks on the ground that looked strange.

There were also a few drops of red on the grass.

Holmes found a small piece of silver caught on a plant.

He asked many questions about the people who worked with Silver Blaze.

Holmes learned that a man named Fitzroy Simpson had visited the night before.

Simpson had asked many questions about Silver Blaze.

He even offered Hunter money, but Hunter said no.

Later that night, Hunter felt very tired and fell to the floor.

Holmes also spoke to a local man named Silas Brown.

Brown worked with horses at a farm not far from the village.

Brown was known to be a hard man.

He had argued with Colonel Ross before.

Holmes saw that Brown’s shoes had the same marks as those near the stable.

Brown tried to explain, but Holmes did not believe him.

The next day, Holmes and Watson walked near the farm where Brown worked.

They saw a group of horses in a field.

One of the horses looked very much like Silver Blaze.

Holmes smiled and said to Watson, "I think we have found Silver Blaze."

The horse had been painted to look different.

But Holmes knew it was Silver Blaze because of the marks he had seen.

Holmes explained that Silas Brown had taken Silver Blaze.

Brown wanted to stop Silver Blaze from running in the race.

He hoped another horse would win instead.

Holmes had seen through the trick and followed the signs to find Silver Blaze.

Colonel Ross was very happy when he saw his horse again.

Silver Blaze was safe and could run in the race after all.

On the day of the race, many people came to watch.

Silver Blaze ran faster than he ever had before.

He won the race easily, and everyone was happy for him.

Colonel Ross was proud of his horse. He thanked Holmes for his help.

Holmes and Watson left the village feeling good about what they had done.

As they walked away, Holmes said something odd to Watson.

He said, "The most important sign was the thing that did not happen."

Watson did not understand, but he knew Holmes was very smart.

They continued their walk, happy to have solved the problem.

Colonel Ross and the people of the village celebrated Silver Blaze’s win.

The horse was safe, and everything had ended well.

The race was over, but people would remember Silver Blaze’s speed.

They would talk about this day for a long time.

Holmes and Watson had done a good job, and they knew it.

The mystery was solved, and everyone was happy with the result.

Silver Blaze was back where he belonged, and the village was quiet again.

The story of Silver Blaze would be told to children for many years.

It was a story of a fast horse, a smart man, and a happy ending.

Colonel Ross often checked on Silver Blaze after that night.

He wanted to make sure the horse was always safe.

The police kept an eye on Silas Brown for a while.

They made sure he did not cause any more trouble.

Hunter recovered from his injury and went back to work.

He was careful to lock the stable door every night.

Fitzroy Simpson was questioned by the police.

They wanted to know if he had anything to do with the horse’s disappearance.

Simpson said he only wanted to learn about Silver Blaze for a friend.

The police did not find any proof that he was involved.

Holmes believed Simpson was not part of the plan.

He thought Simpson was just curious about the horse.

The village held a small party to celebrate Silver Blaze’s win.

People brought food and talked about the exciting race.

Colonel Ross gave a speech about how proud he was of his horse.

He also thanked Holmes and Watson for their help.

Silver Blaze was given extra food and care after the race.

He seemed to enjoy all the attention he was getting.

The stable was made stronger to keep Silver Blaze safe.

Colonel Ross hired more people to watch over the horse.

Holmes and Watson stayed in the village for a few more days.

They wanted to make sure everything was settled before they left.

Watson wrote down the details of the case in his notebook.

He knew it was a story many people would want to read.

Holmes spent time walking around the village.

He enjoyed the quiet and the fresh air.

One evening, Holmes and Watson sat by the fire in their room.

Holmes explained the case to Watson in more detail.

He talked about how he had noticed the strange marks on the ground.

He also explained why the dog did not bark that night.

Watson was surprised to hear this. He had not thought about the dog.

Holmes said that the dog knew the person who took Silver Blaze.

That was why it did not bark or make any noise.

Watson realized how smart Holmes was to notice such a small detail.

The next morning, Holmes and Watson prepared to leave.

Colonel Ross came to say goodbye and thank them again.

He gave them a small gift as a token of his thanks.

Holmes and Watson were happy to have helped the Colonel.

They left the village knowing they had done a great thing.

As they traveled back, Watson thought about the case.

He realized how much he had learned from Holmes.

Holmes noticed Watson’s interest and smiled.

He was glad to have such a good friend and partner.

The two men continued their journey, ready for their next adventure.

### C1
The village of King's Pyland lay curled beneath the South Downs like a sleeping cat, its thatched roofs silvered by the moon and its narrow lanes breathing the damp, sweet scent of new-mown hay. Here, where the chalk hills rolled into the sea, horses were not mere animals; they were the pulse of the land, the measure of a man's standing, the whisper of fortune itself. And of all the horses that ever lifted their hooves across these fields, none shone brighter than Silver Blaze-his coat a cascade of liquid mercury that caught the light like scattered coins, his stride so effortless it seemed he trod upon the very wind. Colonel Ross, a man whose pride was as broad as his moustache was bristling, had staked everything on the great race that loomed seven days hence, and Silver Blaze was the arrow in his quiver.

On the evening before the week's end, the Colonel made his customary rounds, the leather soles of his boots whispering against the cobblestones as he turned toward the stable yard. The air was thick with the musk of straw and the faint, metallic tang of sweat that clung to the wooden stalls, yet beneath it all lingered something else-an unease that prickled the nape of his neck like the brush of a spider's thread. The stable door, usually barred from within, stood ajar, its padlock dangling like a broken promise. A single lantern guttered in the breeze, casting long, wavering fingers across the threshold, and in their flickering light the Colonel saw Hunter, the stable boy, sprawled upon the flagstones, his face as pale as the belly of a fish.

The boy's breath came in shallow, ragged gasps, his eyelids fluttering like moths trapped beneath glass, and when the Colonel knelt beside him, he caught the faint, sickly sweet reek of laudanum clinging to the lad's collar. A cry for the constable was sent echoing through the village, but by the time the heavy-booted men arrived, the stall that had once housed Silver Blaze yawned empty, its straw trampled into a pattern that spoke of struggle, though no hoofprints led away into the night. The Colonel's hands trembled as he traced the edges of the disturbed bedding; the horse had vanished as completely as if the earth itself had swallowed him whole. The constables fanned out across the village, their lanterns bobbing like fireflies in the dark, but not a soul admitted to having seen the silver stallion pass, and the fields beyond lay undisturbed, the dew unbroken.

At dawn the following morning, the Colonel dispatched a telegram to London, and by midday the train deposited two men upon the platform at King's Pyland station: one tall and gaunt, his sharp features carved from some unyielding wood, the other stouter, his face flushed with the pleasure of a man who has just enjoyed a hearty meal. Sherlock Holmes, for it was he, stepped onto the platform with the precision of a clockwork figure, his keen eyes already scanning the horizon as if the very landscape might yield its secrets to him. Dr. Watson, ever the faithful chronicler, followed at his elbow, notebook in hand, though he knew better than to interrupt the great detective's silent communion with the air itself. The Colonel met them at the station, his face a map of worry, and without a word he led them back to the stable where the mystery had begun.

Holmes moved through the scene like a shadow given purpose, his long fingers brushing the straw, the doorframe, the very edges of the night's silence. He paused at a patch of trampled grass where the earth bore the faint imprint of a shoe-too narrow for a labourer's boot, too deliberate for a casual passer-by-and beside it, half-hidden in the undergrowth, a single silver hair glinted like a thread of moonlight. Nearby, a few drops of crimson marred the green, their edges already darkening to rust, and Holmes lifted one to his nose, his expression unreadable. When he spoke, his voice was low, almost to himself: "The dog did not bark, Watson. That is the curious incident."

The Colonel, overhearing, frowned. "There was no dog here that night, Mr. Holmes. Only Hunter, and he was alone." Holmes merely smiled, a thin, humourless curve of the lips. "Precisely. And yet, had there been a dog, it would have barked at a stranger." His questions turned then to the stable hands, the grooms, the men who mucked out the stalls and knew the rhythms of the place as intimately as their own heartbeats. From them he learned of Fitzroy Simpson, a lean, restless man with a gambler's eyes, who had lingered about the yard the previous evening, his questions about Silver Blaze's training regimen as pointed as the blade of a knife.

Simpson had offered Hunter a sovereign for a private word, but the boy, loyal to the Colonel, had refused-though not before the stranger had pressed a folded note into his palm, its contents unknown. Later, Hunter had complained of a sudden, crushing weariness, his limbs heavy as lead, and had collapsed upon the straw, the note slipping from his fingers like a dying breath. Holmes retrieved the scrap of paper, its edges singed as if held too close to a flame, and read aloud the single line scrawled upon it: "The horse will not run." His gaze lingered on the words, then lifted to meet Watson's. "A threat, or a promise?"

The trail led next to the farm of Silas Brown, a man whose reputation was as rough as the callouses on his hands, and whose temper was said to flare as quickly as a struck match. He had quarreled with the Colonel only a fortnight prior, his voice raised in anger over some trifling matter of grazing rights, and though the dispute had been settled, the bitterness lingered like the taste of spoiled wine. Holmes found Brown in the yard, his boots caked with mud that bore the same narrow tread as the prints near the stable, and when the detective's eyes fell upon them, the farmer's face darkened like a sky before a storm. "A man walks where he pleases on his own land," Brown growled, but his hands betrayed him, twisting together as if wringing the neck of some unseen foe.

The next morning, under the guise of a casual stroll, Holmes and Watson made their way to the fields behind Brown's farm, where a group of horses grazed beneath the watchful eye of a surly groom. Among them stood a chestnut mare, her coat dull and unremarkable-save for the faintest shimmer beneath the dirt, like the ghost of a silver sheen that refused to be entirely scrubbed away. Holmes's lips twitched. "Observe, Watson: the ears, the slope of the shoulder, the way the light catches the muscle beneath the skin. That is no mare." He turned to the groom, his voice deceptively mild. "How long has this horse been in your care?" The man's eyes darted like a trapped animal's before he muttered, "Since last night."

The truth unraveled swiftly after that: Brown, desperate to ensure his own horse's victory in the race, had arranged for Silver Blaze to be spirited away under cover of darkness, his distinctive coat dyed to disguise his identity. The laudanum in Hunter's tea had been a precaution, ensuring the boy would not wake to sound the alarm, and the absence of hoofprints had been a clever misdirection, the horse led away by hand along the soft verge where the earth would not hold a mark. When the Colonel arrived to reclaim his champion, his relief was so profound it bordered on reverence, and he clasped Holmes's hand as if it were a lifeline thrown to a drowning man. Silver Blaze, his coat still streaked with the remnants of dye, nuzzled at the Colonel's pocket for the sugar lump he knew would be there, his eyes bright with the unspoken understanding of a creature who had cheated fate itself.

The day of the race dawned clear and bright, the sky a vast, unbroken blue that seemed to promise victory to all who dared to dream it. The stands were packed with spectators, their voices a rising tide of anticipation, and when Silver Blaze stepped onto the track, his silver coat gleaming like polished steel, a hush fell over the crowd as if they, too, sensed the weight of the moment. The race itself was a blur of motion and sound, the thunder of hooves, the roar of the crowd, the whip-crack of the wind as Silver Blaze surged ahead, his legs a blur, his mane streaming behind him like a banner of war. When he crossed the finish line, his lead so great it seemed the other horses stood still, the cheers that erupted were not merely for the winner, but for the triumph of justice, the unraveling of deceit, the return of what had been stolen.

That evening, as Holmes and Watson prepared to depart, the Colonel pressed a small, leather-bound case into the detective's hands. "A token of my gratitude," he said, his voice rough with emotion. Holmes opened it to reveal a silver flask, its surface engraved with the image of a running horse, and though he said nothing, the ghost of a smile touched his lips. Watson, watching, felt the familiar stir of admiration for his friend, whose mind could weave together the threads of a mystery as effortlessly as a spider spins its web. As their train pulled away from the station, Holmes leaned back in his seat and closed his eyes, the flask warm against his palm. "The curious incident of the dog in the night-time, Watson," he murmured. "It did not bark because it knew the thief."

## st-0220 — The Man with the Twisted Lip
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
A man lives near London in 1891. His name is Neville St. Clair. He has a big house. He has a family too.

He works in the city each day. People say he is kind. He leaves home early. He comes home late.

One night, his woman sees something. She goes to the city to buy things. She sees a man in a small house. The man looks like her man.

The man has old black things on. His face looks bad. She thinks it is her man. The man sees her and moves back.

Mrs. St. Clair feels very bad. She calls some men to help. The men go to the house. They do not find her man.

They find a man named Hugh Boone. Boone’s face looks bad. He sits on the ground. He says he does not see Mr. St. Clair.

He says he sees no one. The men do not believe Mrs. St. Clair. They leave the house. Mrs. St. Clair knows her man.

He would not have old things on. She asks her friend Sherlock Holmes. Holmes hears her story. He says he will look at the case.

He goes to the house with Dr. Watson. They find Boone on the same ground. Holmes looks around the room. He sees something not right.

He asks Boone many things. Boone does not give clear answers. Holmes takes Boone’s black thing off. Under it is Mr. St. Clair’s face.

St. Clair says he lives two lives. Long ago, he writes about asking for money. He tries it for one day. He makes more money than at his job.

He keeps asking for money. He has old things on. He makes his face look bad. He puts a thing on his head.

His woman sees him and he feels bad. He tries to stop her. It is too late. He tells Holmes everything.

Holmes says he will not tell the men. But St. Clair must stop asking for money. St. Clair goes home the next day. He lives with his family again.

No one sees Hugh Boone after that. The case is over.

### A2
In 1891, a man lived near London. His name was Neville St. Clair. He had a big house and a kind family.

He worked in the city each day. He left home early in the morning. He came back late at night. His family did not worry about him.

One evening, his wife went to the city. She wanted to buy food for the family. She saw a man at a window of a small house.

The man looked like her husband. But he wore old clothes. His face looked different.

She felt sure it was him. But the man moved back fast. He did it when he saw her.

Mrs. St. Clair felt very bad. She called the police for help.

The police went to the house. But the man was not there. They found only a poor man called Hugh Boone.

Boone had the same different face. He sat on the floor. He said nothing to the police. He told them he did not see Mr. St. Clair.

The police did not believe Mrs. St. Clair. They left the house.

Mrs. St. Clair knew her husband would not wear old clothes. She asked her friend Sherlock Holmes for help.

Holmes listened to her story. He said he was going to look into the case. He and his friend Dr. Watson went to the house.

They found Boone still on the floor. Holmes saw something odd in the room.

Holmes asked Boone many questions. Boone did not give clear answers.

Then Holmes pulled Boone’s hair. Under the hair was Neville St. Clair’s face.

St. Clair told his story. He said he lived two lives. He worked in the city each day.

He also asked for money on the street. Years ago, he wrote about asking for money. He tried it for one day.

He made more money than at his job. He kept asking for money. He wore old clothes and made his face look different. No one knew it was him.

When his wife saw him, he tried to move away. But it was too late. She already saw him.

St. Clair promised to stop asking for money. Holmes said he would not tell the police. But St. Clair must give up his second life.

The next day, St. Clair went home. No one ever saw Hugh Boone again. Everyone felt happy.

The story shows how one man had two lives. It was hard for him to keep them separate. In the end, he chose to live only one life.

### B1
In the year 1891, a man called Neville St. Clair lived in a large house near London. He worked in the city and was a good and kind person.

Every morning, he left home early and came back late. His family never worried about him because he always returned safely.

One evening, his wife went to the city to buy things for the house. She saw something that surprised her a lot.

At a window of a small house, she saw a man who looked like her husband. But he wore old clothes and his face seemed different.

The man moved his mouth in a strange way. Mrs. St. Clair felt sure it was him.

When he saw her, he pulled back from the window quickly. She felt very sad and worried.

Mrs. St. Clair called the police and told them what she saw. They went to the house with her to check.

When they arrived, the man was not there. Inside, they found only a poor man named Hugh Boone.

Boone had a strange mouth and sat in a chair. He said he had not seen Mr. St. Clair that day.

The police did not believe Mrs. St. Clair. They thought she made a mistake and left the house.

Mrs. St. Clair knew her husband would never wear such clothes. She decided to ask her friend Sherlock Holmes for help.

Holmes listened to her story with care. He agreed to look into the matter right away.

Holmes and his friend Dr. Watson went to the small house. They found Boone still sitting in the same chair.

Holmes looked around the room and noticed something odd. He asked Boone many questions, but Boone did not answer clearly.

Then Holmes quickly pulled at Boone’s hair. The hair came off, and under it was Neville St. Clair’s face.

St. Clair admitted he had been living two lives. He explained how it all started many years before.

He once wrote a piece about people who ask for money on the street. To understand better, he tried it himself for one day.

He was surprised to make more money that way than at his job. So he decided to keep doing it in secret.

He wore old clothes and made his face look different. This way, no one would know who he really was.

But when his wife saw him, he knew his secret was in trouble. He tried to stop her, but it was too late.

St. Clair promised to stop and return to his old life. Holmes agreed not to tell the police if St. Clair kept his promise.

The next day, St. Clair went home to his family. No one ever saw Hugh Boone again after that.

The case was solved, and everyone felt happy about the result. Mrs. St. Clair was glad to have her husband back.

Holmes and Watson were pleased they could help. St. Clair learned his lesson and never did it again.

His family was happy to have things return to normal. They all lived together without any more secrets.

Mrs. St. Clair often thought about what had happened. She was glad her husband was safe at home now.

Holmes told Watson that people sometimes do strange things for money. But St. Clair had a good heart and would not do it again.

The police never found out about the secret. They still thought Mrs. St. Clair had made a mistake.

St. Clair was careful never to go near that small house again. He wanted to keep his family happy and safe.

His children never knew about his secret life. They only knew their father as a good and kind man.

Years later, Mrs. St. Clair sometimes smiled when she remembered the day. She was glad Holmes had helped them so quickly.

St. Clair worked hard at his job and was happy at home. He never wanted to risk losing his family again.

Holmes and Watson often talked about the case. It was one of the most unusual they had ever solved.

The small house where Boone sat was empty after that. No one else ever lived there in the same way.

St. Clair’s life was back to normal, and he was glad. He knew he had made a big mistake but was lucky to fix it.

### B2
In the year eighteen ninety-one, a man called Neville St. Clair lived in a large house near London. He worked in the city and was known as a kind and honest person.

Every morning, he left home early and came back late. His family never felt worry about him because he always returned safely.

One evening, his wife went to the city to buy things. While she was walking, she saw something that surprised her.

She noticed a man at a window of a small house. The man looked very much like her husband, but he wore old clothes and had a twisted mouth.

She was sure it was him, but he quickly moved away from the window when he saw her. Mrs. St. Clair felt very upset and decided to call the police.

The police came to the house, but the man was no longer there. Inside, they found only a poor man named Hugh Boone.

Boone had a twisted mouth and was sitting in a chair. He said he had not seen Mr. St. Clair, so the police did not believe Mrs. St. Clair.

They left, but Mrs. St. Clair knew her husband would never wear such clothes. She thought about the situation and decided to ask her friend Sherlock Holmes for help.

Holmes listened to her story carefully and agreed to look into the matter. He and his friend Dr. Watson went to the house where Boone lived.

They found Boone still sitting in the same chair. Holmes noticed something strange about the room and began to ask Boone questions.

Boone did not answer clearly, so Holmes suddenly took hold of Boone’s hair and pulled. The hair came off, and under it was Neville St. Clair’s face!

St. Clair admitted that he had been living two lives. He explained that years ago, he wrote an article about people who ask for money on the street.

To understand better, he tried asking for money himself for one day. He was surprised to find that he made more money that way than at his job.

He decided to keep asking for money in secret while still living as a respected man. He wore old clothes and used makeup to hide who he really was.

But when his wife saw him, he knew his secret was in danger. He tried to stop her, but it was too late.

St. Clair promised to stop asking for money and go back to his old life. Holmes agreed not to tell the police if St. Clair gave up his double life.

The next day, St. Clair returned home to his family. No one ever saw Hugh Boone again, and the matter was settled.

Mrs. St. Clair felt happy that her husband was back. She was glad the strange situation was over and they could live normally again.

Holmes and Watson were pleased with how the case ended. They knew St. Clair would keep his promise and return to his honest life.

The family continued to live in their large house near London. St. Clair went to work every day, and no one ever spoke of the strange event again.

Years passed, and St. Clair remained a kind and honest man. His wife and children were proud of him, and he was happy to be part of a loving family.

Holmes often thought about the case and how people can surprise you. He knew that even the most honest person might do strange things for money.

But in the end, St. Clair made the right choice. He chose his family and his good name over the easy money he could have made.

The story shows that it is important to be honest and true to yourself. Even when life is hard, doing the right thing will always bring peace in the end.

Mrs. St. Clair was glad she had trusted her eyes that evening. If she had not seen her husband in that window, his secret might never have been discovered.

Holmes was happy to help, as he always enjoyed solving difficult cases. He knew that every case taught him something new about people and life.

St. Clair’s children grew up to be good and honest, just like their father. They never knew about their father’s secret life, and that was for the best.

The family lived a quiet and happy life after that. They were grateful for each other and the peace they had found.

The case of Neville St. Clair became one of Holmes’s most interesting cases. It showed that even the most ordinary people can have surprising secrets.

In the end, everyone was glad that the truth had come out. St. Clair was able to return to his normal life, and his family was happy to have him back.

Holmes and Watson often talked about the case over the years. They agreed that it was one of the most unusual cases they had ever solved.

St. Clair kept his promise and never asked for money again. He worked hard at his job and provided well for his family.

His wife was always by his side, supporting him and loving him. Together, they built a happy life and put the strange event behind them.

The story of Neville St. Clair is a reminder that honesty is always the best choice. Even when it is hard, doing what is right will bring happiness in the end.

### C1
The autumn of eighteen ninety-one settled over the Kentish countryside like a velvet shroud, its chill fingers creeping through the cracks of even the most stoutly built homes. Neville St. Clair's residence-a red-brick manor with ivy-clawed gables-stood sentinel over the winding lane that led to London, its windows glowing amber in the dusk as if the very house exhaled warmth. Inside, the scent of beeswax and old books mingled with the faint tang of pipe tobacco, a fragrance Mrs. St. Clair had come to associate with safety, with routine, with the unshakable certainty that her husband would walk through the door at half past seven, his coat damp from the evening mist, his smile as steady as the ticking of the grandfather clock in the hall. For years, this rhythm had been their life: his departure at dawn, his return at dusk, the quiet domesticity of meals shared and children tucked into bed, all underpinned by an unspoken trust that Neville St. Clair was exactly the man he appeared to be-kind, dependable, utterly without guile.

That trust was not born of naivety, but of observation. Mrs. St. Clair had watched her husband navigate the world with a quiet dignity, his hand extended to a beggar as readily as to a banker, his voice never raised in anger, his pockets never empty when a child's coin was lost in the street. The city knew him too; clerks tipped their hats, newsboys waved, and even the stern-faced constables at the station nodded in recognition as he passed, briefcase in hand, his gait unhurried but purposeful. It was this very reputation that made the sighting on that October evening so impossible to reconcile with the man she knew-or thought she knew.

The errand that took her into London had been a simple one: a bolt of silk for her daughter's birthday dress, a pound of tea, a new pair of gloves to replace the ones she'd misplaced that morning. The streets were slick with rain, the gas lamps casting long, wavering fingers of light that turned the cobblestones into a mosaic of gold and shadow. She had paused beneath the awning of a milliner's shop to adjust her umbrella when the movement caught her eye-a flicker of motion in the upper window of a narrow, soot-stained house across the alley. The glass was grimy, the curtains thin, but the face that pressed close to it for a single, heart-stopping moment was unmistakably her husband's, though twisted into an expression she had never seen him wear: a grimace of such cunning, such desperation, that it might have belonged to a stranger.

The shock rooted her to the spot, her breath fogging the air between them. For a wild instant, she wondered if her eyes had deceived her-if the play of light, the fatigue of the day, had conspired to create a phantom where none existed. But then the face jerked back, the curtains twitched shut, and the certainty settled over her like a shroud: that had been Neville, and Neville had been hiding. The question that clawed at her was not whether he had been there, but why-a question that grew teeth as she crossed the alley, her skirts brushing against the damp brick, and rapped sharply on the door of the house.

No answer came, though she thought she heard the scuffle of footsteps above. A neighbor, a gaunt woman with a shawl pulled tight over her shoulders, leaned out of the adjacent window and hissed, "Boone's not one for visitors, missus." The name meant nothing to her, but the warning in the woman's voice was unmistakable. It was only when the police arrived-summoned by a trembling hand and a voice that barely rose above a whisper-that the truth began to unravel, thread by fragile thread.

The constables found no sign of Neville St. Clair inside the house, only a man slumped in a chair by the window, his face half-hidden beneath a tangle of greasy hair, his mouth twisted into a permanent sneer. Hugh Boone, they called him, a beggar of some renown in the district, known for his sharp tongue and sharper elbows when the alms grew scarce. He claimed no knowledge of any gentleman caller, his voice a guttural rasp that seemed to scrape against the walls, but Mrs. St. Clair's gaze lingered on the chair where he sat, on the way his fingers twitched toward a pile of rags in the corner, as if something there might incriminate him. The police, satisfied with his denials and her apparent hysteria, left with a patronizing pat on her shoulder, but the seed of doubt they planted had already taken root.

That night, she penned a letter to Sherlock Holmes, the words spilling onto the page in a frantic scrawl, each sentence a plea, a prayer, a desperate attempt to make sense of the impossible. By morning, the reply had come: a single line, inked in Holmes's unmistakable hand, promising his attendance that very afternoon. When he arrived, his keen eyes missed nothing-the way her teacup trembled in its saucer, the shadows beneath her eyes, the way her voice caught when she described the twisted mouth at the window. Watson, ever the observer of human frailty, noted the way her hands clenched in her lap, as if physically restraining herself from reaching for the truth too soon.

The house on the alley smelled of damp and decay, the air thick with the sour reek of unwashed clothes and something sharper-fear, perhaps, or guilt. Boone was still there, still slumped in his chair, his eyes half-lidded as if the world were too dull to merit his full attention. Holmes circled him like a hawk, his gaze dissecting every detail: the callouses on Boone's fingers, the way his left leg jiggled with nervous energy, the faint smear of greasepaint at his hairline. Then, without warning, he lunged, his fingers closing around Boone's hair-and with a single, violent tug, the wig came away, revealing the smooth, familiar brow of Neville St. Clair beneath.

The confession that followed was not one of defiance, but of exhaustion. Years earlier, St. Clair had written an article on the economics of begging, a piece meant to expose the desperation of those who lived on the streets. To research it, he had disguised himself as a beggar for a single day, expecting pity, perhaps, or scorn-but never the flood of coins that clattered into his upturned hat, more than he earned in a week at his respectable desk. The temptation had been too great, the secret too intoxicating: a double life, one of respectability by day, of easy riches by night, all hidden beneath the rags and grime of Hugh Boone.

Holmes listened in silence, his expression unreadable, as St. Clair spoke of the fear that had gnawed at him-the fear of discovery, of ruin, of the life he had built crumbling like ash in his hands. When the tale was done, Holmes offered no judgment, only a bargain: the secret would remain between them, so long as St. Clair abandoned his alter ego for good. The relief on St. Clair's face was palpable, a weight lifted, though the shadow of what he had done would never fully leave him. As they left the house, Watson glanced back once, half-expecting to see Boone's twisted grin lingering in the window, but the alley was empty, the curtain still.

The return to normalcy was not immediate, nor was it seamless. St. Clair's children noticed the way he sometimes stared into the middle distance, his hands stilling mid-motion, as if listening for a voice that no longer called. His wife, though she never spoke of it, found herself studying his face in the lamplight, searching for traces of the man she had glimpsed in that grimy window. But time, as it does, softened the edges of memory, and soon the rhythm of their lives resumed: the morning departures, the evening returns, the quiet domesticity that had once seemed so unremarkable now a gift to be cherished.

Holmes, for his part, filed the case away with a satisfaction that bordered on melancholy. It was not the most dramatic of his investigations, nor the most dangerous, but it lingered in his mind like the aftertaste of a fine wine-complex, bittersweet, impossible to forget. Watson, ever the chronicler, would later write of it as a tale of redemption, of a man who had strayed and found his way home. But Holmes knew the truth was simpler, and far more human: St. Clair had chosen, in the end, not between good and evil, but between two versions of himself-and in that choice, he had found a kind of peace.

## st-0221 — The Musgrave Ritual
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
A man calls Holmes. He needs help. His name is Reginald Musgrave.

Musgrave has a family problem. His family has a special paper. It is the Musgrave paper.

The paper has questions. Every first child must answer them. Musgrave does not know what it means.

He says he never thinks about it. A man is not there. His name is Brunton.

Brunton reads the paper at night. He asks about old family things. Musgrave sees him.

Musgrave is not good with this. He tells Brunton to go. Brunton asks to stay a few days.

The next day Brunton is not there. His bed is not used. His things are still in the house.

Musgrave looks for Brunton. He finds nothing. A woman feels very bad.

Her name is Rachel Howells. She does not want to see Brunton. Holmes hears Musgrave.

He asks to see the paper. The paper says words. 'Whose was it? His who is gone.'

'Who will have it? He who will come.' Holmes says it shows a place. He goes to Musgrave’s house.

They look at a big old thing. Holmes finds a special place. He says the answer is under a part.

He says it is under the old part. They go to a room down. It is not light and has old things.

They find a small thing. It is under some hard parts. It is an old thing from a leader.

Holmes says the paper helps keep it. The questions help find it. Only the first child can know them.

Brunton wants the old thing. Holmes finds Brunton’s body. He is in a small room.

The door closes and stops him. Rachel knows what happens. She feels very bad and runs away.

The problem is over. Musgrave is good to have the old thing. He feels bad for Brunton.

He puts the old thing in a place. It is a good place. Holmes says some family things have bad parts.

The bad parts can be not good. The Musgrave paper is one of them.

### A2
Long ago, my friend Holmes told me a story. It was not his normal work.

A man came to see Holmes one night. His name was Reginald Musgrave. He looked worried and needed help.

Musgrave told Holmes about his family’s old rule. For many years, the oldest son had to do something. He had to answer some questions.

This was called the Musgrave Rule. It was on a piece of paper. The questions were always the same.

No one knew what they really meant. Musgrave said, 'I never thought about it before.'

He told Holmes his worker, Brunton, was gone. Before he left, Brunton acted in a different way. He read the Rule late at night.

He asked about old family history. Musgrave saw him look at the paper. He told Brunton to leave the house.

Brunton asked to stay a few more days. The next morning, Brunton was not there. His bed was not used.

His things were still in his room. Musgrave looked but found nothing. Then a woman named Rachel Howells felt bad.

She was close to Brunton. She did not want to see him again. Holmes listened and asked to see the Rule.

The paper said: 'Whose was it? His who is gone.' It also said: 'Who shall have it? He who will come.' It talked about a tree and a place.

Holmes thought for a moment. He said, 'This is not just a rule—it shows a place.' He decided to go to Musgrave’s house.

When they got there, Holmes looked at an old tree. He measured its shade to find a place on the ground. Then he asked about the house’s history.

He learned part of the house was very old. Holmes said, 'The answer is under the old part.' They went to a room below the house.

It was dark and not clean. They found a small box under some rocks. Inside was an old thing for the head.

It was for a leader long ago. Holmes said the Rule kept the thing safe. The questions helped find it.

Only the oldest son could understand them. Brunton knew the answer and wanted the thing. Holmes then found Brunton’s body.

Brunton went to take the thing. He got stuck when the door closed. Rachel Howells knew what happened.

She felt so bad that she left. The problem was over. It was a bad end for Brunton.

Musgrave was happy to have the thing back. But he felt sorry for what happened. Holmes said some family rules hide more than words.

They can hide things that are better not found. The Musgrave Rule was one of those things. It led to a bad end.

After that day, Musgrave kept the thing in a better place. Holmes never forgot how simple questions could change lives.

### B1
Many years ago, my friend Sherlock Holmes told me about a strange event.

It was not like his usual work.

A man named Reginald Musgrave came to see Holmes one evening in London.

He looked worried and said he needed help.

Musgrave told Holmes about his family’s old custom.

For many years, the oldest son in the family had to answer some questions.

This was called the Musgrave Ritual.

It was written on a piece of paper.

The questions were always the same.

No one knew what they meant.

Musgrave said, 'I never thought about it much before now.'

He told Holmes that a man who worked for him, Brunton, had left.

Before he left, Brunton acted in a strange way.

Musgrave saw him reading the Ritual late at night.

Brunton also asked questions about old family history.

Musgrave found him looking at the paper and got angry.

He told Brunton to leave the house.

Brunton asked to stay for a few more days.

Musgrave said yes.

The next morning, Brunton was gone.

His bed had not been slept in.

His things were still in his room.

Musgrave looked everywhere but found nothing.

Then, a woman named Rachel Howells became very ill.

She had been close to Brunton.

She cried all the time and said she never wanted to see him again.

Holmes listened and asked to see the Ritual.

The paper had these words: 'Whose was it? His who is gone.'

'Who shall have it? He who will come.'

It also talked about a tree and a place where something was hidden.

Holmes thought for a moment.

He said, 'This is not just a custom—it is like a map.'

He decided to go to Musgrave’s house right away.

When they arrived, Holmes looked at the big tree.

It was very tall.

He measured the ground near it to find a special spot.

Then he asked Musgrave about the house’s past.

Holmes learned that part of the house was very old.

He said, 'The answer must be under the old part of the house.'

They went to a room below the house.

It was dark and had not been used for a long time.

In that room, they found a small, heavy box under some stones.

Inside was an old gold thing that had belonged to a king long ago.

Holmes explained that the Ritual was a way to keep the gold thing safe.

The questions helped find it.

Only the family’s oldest son could understand them.

Brunton had figured it out and wanted the gold thing for himself.

Then Holmes found Brunton’s body in a small room near the old part.

He had gone there to take the gold thing.

The door closed and he could not get out.

Rachel Howells knew what happened.

She felt so bad that she ran away.

The event was solved, but it was a sad end for Brunton.

Musgrave was glad to have the gold thing back.

But he also felt sorry.

Holmes told me that some family customs have more than just words.

They can hide things that are better left alone.

The Musgrave Ritual was one of those things.

It led to a sad end.

After that, Musgrave kept the gold thing in a safer place.

Holmes never forgot how a simple set of questions could change lives.

He often thought about it when he worked on other cases.

Sometimes, old customs hold secrets no one should find.

Musgrave learned that the hard way.

He told his son about the Ritual when he was older.

He wanted to make sure the same thing would not happen again.

The son promised to be careful with the family’s past.

Holmes and I talked about the case many times after that.

We agreed that some things are better left hidden.

The gold thing stayed safe, but the memory of Brunton did not.

Musgrave never forgot the man who tried to take what was not his.

He also never forgot the woman who ran away because of it.

The house stayed quiet after that.

No one talked about the Ritual much.

But everyone knew it was still there, waiting.

Waiting for the next person who might try to solve it.

### B2
Many years ago, my friend Sherlock Holmes told me about a strange event he worked on. It was not like his usual cases.

It began with an old family trouble. A man called Reginald Musgrave came to see Holmes one evening in London.

Musgrave looked troubled and said he needed help with something very important. He explained his family had a special rule.

For hundreds of years, every oldest son in the family had to answer a set of questions. This was called the Musgrave Custom, and it was written on paper.

The questions were always the same, but no one knew what they truly meant. Musgrave said, 'I never gave it much thought until now.'

He told Holmes that a man who worked for him, named Brunton, had left. Before he left, Brunton had acted in a strange way.

He was seen reading the Custom late at night and asking about old family history. Musgrave caught him looking at the paper and became angry.

He told Brunton to leave the house, but Brunton asked to stay for a few more days. The next morning, Brunton was gone.

His bed had not been slept in, and his things were still in his room. Musgrave searched everywhere in the house and garden but found nothing.

Then, a woman who worked for him, named Rachel Howells, became very upset. She had been close to Brunton and cried all the time.

She said she never wanted to see him again. Holmes listened carefully and asked to see the Custom.

The paper had these words: 'Whose was it? His who is gone.', 'Who shall have it? He who will come.' It also spoke of a tree and a place where something was kept.

Holmes thought for a moment and said, 'This is not just a rule—it is like a guide.' He decided to go to Musgrave’s house at once to solve the trouble.

When they arrived, Holmes looked at the tall tree mentioned in the Custom. He measured its shadow to find a special spot on the ground.

Then he asked Musgrave about the history of the house. He learned that part of it was very old.

Holmes said, 'The answer must be under the old part of the house.' They went to a dark room below the house that was full of dust.

In that room, they found a small, heavy box hidden under some stones. Inside was an old gold circle that had belonged to a king long ago.

Holmes explained that the Custom was a way to keep it safe. The questions were like signs to find it, and only the family’s oldest son could understand them.

Brunton had figured it out and wanted it for himself. Holmes then found Brunton’s body in a small room near the dark room below.

He had gone there to take the gold circle but got trapped when the door closed behind him. Rachel Howells knew what had happened and was so upset that she ran away.

The trouble was solved, but it was a bad end for Brunton. Musgrave was glad to have the gold circle back but felt sorry for what had happened.

Holmes told me that some family rules hold more than just words. They can hide secrets that might be better if they stayed hidden.

The Musgrave Custom was one of those secrets, and it led to a very bad end. From that day on, Musgrave kept the gold circle in a safer place.

Holmes never forgot how a simple set of questions could change lives so much. If Brunton had not been so curious, he might still be alive today.

Rachel Howells, who was very upset, could have told someone what she knew. If she had, Brunton’s life might have been saved.

The Musgrave family had kept the Custom for many years without understanding it. They did not know it would lead to such a bad event.

Holmes often thought about how traditions can have hidden parts. He believed that some secrets should remain secrets.

This case showed him that even old family rules can cause big problems. It was a lesson he never forgot.

Musgrave, who was glad to have the gold circle back, also felt a lot of sorrow. He wished he had understood the Custom’s true purpose earlier.

If he had, perhaps Brunton would not have tried to take the gold circle. The whole bad event might have been avoided.

Holmes and I talked about the case many times after that. We both agreed that some things are better left unknown.

The Musgrave Custom was one of those things. It was a secret that brought only sorrow and loss.

Many years have passed since that case, but I still remember it clearly. It was one of the most unusual events Holmes ever worked on.

The Custom seemed simple, but it held a dangerous secret. No one in the family had realized how much trouble it could cause.

Brunton’s curiosity led him to discover the gold circle’s hiding place. He thought he could take it without anyone knowing.

But the room where it was hidden was old and dangerous. The door closed behind him, and he could not get out.

Rachel Howells knew what had happened but was too upset to speak. She ran away and was never seen again.

Musgrave felt responsible for what had happened. He wished he had paid more attention to the Custom earlier.

Holmes said that some family traditions are not as harmless as they seem. They can hide things that are better left alone.

The gold circle was now safe, but the cost had been very high. A man had lost his life because of it.

Holmes believed that if people knew the truth about some secrets, they might not want to find them. The Musgrave Custom was a perfect example of this.

From that day forward, Musgrave made sure the gold circle was kept in a secure place. He did not want any more trouble to come from it.

Holmes and I often wondered what would have happened if Brunton had not been so curious. Perhaps he would still be alive, and Rachel would not have run away.

But some people cannot resist the chance to find something valuable. Brunton was one of those people, and it cost him his life.

The Musgrave Custom had been kept for generations without anyone understanding its true purpose. It was only when Brunton tried to solve it that the trouble began.

Holmes said that some secrets are meant to stay hidden. The gold circle was one of those secrets, and it should have remained so.

Musgrave agreed, and he never spoke of the Custom again. He wanted to forget the whole bad event.

But Holmes and I knew that some things cannot be forgotten. The memory of Brunton and Rachel stayed with us for a long time.

It was a case that taught us both a valuable lesson. Some secrets are better left alone, no matter how curious we might be.

### C1
The case began, as so many did, in the dim glow of a London evening, when the gas lamps flickered like restless spirits against the encroaching fog. Sherlock Holmes sat by the fire, his long fingers steepled beneath his chin, while I nursed a glass of whisky that had long since lost its warmth. It was not the usual clatter of hansom cabs or the distant hum of the city that drew his attention, but the hesitant knock at our door-a sound so faint it might have been the wind testing the latch. When I opened it, Reginald Musgrave stood there, his overcoat damp with rain, his face a landscape of unease, as though the very air had conspired to weigh him down.

Musgrave was a man of quiet bearing, the kind who carried his lineage like an invisible cloak, yet tonight his usual composure had frayed at the edges. He spoke in measured tones, but his hands betrayed him, twisting the brim of his hat into something unrecognizable. The trouble, he explained, was not of the present but of the past-a past enshrined in a ritual so old it had become little more than a whisper in the halls of Hurlstone Manor. For centuries, the eldest son of the Musgrave family had been required to recite the Musgrave Ritual, a cryptic litany of questions and answers passed down like a relic no one dared to examine too closely.

Holmes leaned forward, his eyes sharp with interest, for there was nothing he relished more than a puzzle wrapped in the dust of history. Musgrave produced a yellowed sheet of parchment, its edges brittle with age, and read aloud the words that had haunted his family for generations: *Whose was it? His who is gone. Who shall have it? He who will come.* The phrases were simple, almost childlike, yet they carried the weight of something far darker, as though the words themselves were a key turning in a lock no one had thought to open. Musgrave confessed he had never given the Ritual much thought-until his butler, Brunton, had begun to unravel its secrets with a fervor that bordered on obsession.

Brunton was a man of keen intellect, a scholar in servant's livery, and for years he had moved through Hurlstone like a shadow, efficient and unobtrusive. But lately, Musgrave had noticed a change in him-a restlessness, a hunger in his eyes when he thought no one was looking. One night, Musgrave had found him poring over the Ritual by candlelight, his fingers tracing the words as though they might dissolve beneath his touch. When confronted, Brunton had begged for time, claiming he was merely satisfying a curiosity that had gnawed at him for months, but Musgrave, sensing something amiss, had dismissed him on the spot.

The next morning, Brunton was gone, vanished as though the earth had swallowed him whole. His bed had not been slept in, his belongings remained untouched, and the only trace of him was the faint scent of pipe tobacco lingering in the air, as though he had stepped out for a moment and never returned. Musgrave searched the estate from the attics to the cellars, but there was no sign of him-not a footprint in the dew, not a whisper in the corridors. Then, Rachel Howells, the housemaid and Brunton's former confidante, had collapsed into hysterical sobs, her grief so raw it seemed to scrape the very walls of the house.

Holmes listened to this tale with the stillness of a predator tracking its prey, his mind already sifting through the fragments for a pattern. He asked to see the Ritual again, his gaze lingering on the final lines: *Under the shadow of the great elm, where the stones of the old keep lie, there it waits for him who shall come.* The elm, Musgrave explained, was a towering sentinel at the edge of the estate, its branches gnarled with age, its roots twisting deep into the earth like the fingers of some ancient beast. Holmes nodded, as though this were the piece he had been waiting for, and declared they must leave for Hurlstone at once-for the answers, he was certain, lay buried beneath its stones.

The journey to Sussex was a blur of rain-slicked roads and the rhythmic clatter of the train, the landscape outside the window a watercolor of greys and greens. When they arrived at Hurlstone, the manor loomed before them, its ivy-choked walls whispering of centuries gone by, its windows like hollow eyes watching their approach. Holmes wasted no time; he strode to the elm, his boots sinking into the damp earth, and measured the length of its shadow with the precision of a mathematician. Then, with Musgrave in tow, he followed the line to a patch of ground where the grass grew thin over weathered stones-stones that marked the foundation of the old keep, long since swallowed by time.

Beneath those stones, they found a trapdoor, its iron ring rusted with disuse, its hinges groaning like a dying man as Musgrave heaved it open. A narrow staircase descended into darkness, the air thick with the scent of damp and something older, something metallic and faintly sweet. Holmes lit a lantern, its flickering light casting monstrous shadows on the walls, and together they stepped into the bowels of the house. The cellar was a labyrinth of forgotten things-crates of mildewed books, broken furniture, and in the far corner, a small iron-bound chest, its surface pitted with age.

The chest was heavier than it looked, its lock stubborn, but Holmes worked at it with a thin blade until it yielded with a sharp *click*. Inside lay a circlet of gold, its surface dull with tarnish, its edges adorned with the faint imprint of a crown-a relic, Holmes surmised, of some long-dead king, hidden away by the Musgraves for reasons lost to history. Musgrave stared at it, his breath shallow, as though he had unearthed not a treasure but a ghost. Then Holmes's lantern caught something else-a dark shape huddled in the corner, half-buried beneath a pile of rotting sacks.

It was Brunton. His face was pale, his lips parted as though he had died mid-sentence, his fingers still curled around the edge of a loose stone in the wall. Holmes knelt beside him, his expression unreadable, and after a moment, he stood and turned to Musgrave with a grim set to his mouth. The butler had solved the Ritual, he explained, and come here to claim the treasure, but the mechanism of the door-a cleverly concealed latch-had trapped him inside, leaving him to suffocate in the dark.

Musgrave's face drained of color, his hands trembling as he clutched the gold circlet to his chest. Holmes's voice was quiet as he spoke of Rachel Howells, of how she must have known what had happened, of how her grief had driven her to flee into the night, never to be seen again. The Ritual, he said, had been a test-a test of loyalty, of discretion-but Brunton's ambition had turned it into a death sentence. Musgrave nodded, his throat working as though the words had lodged there, and for a long moment, the only sound was the distant drip of water from the cellar's ceiling.

In the days that followed, Musgrave had the circlet locked away in a bank vault, its location known only to himself and his solicitor. He never spoke of the Ritual again, and the questions it posed were left to gather dust in the annals of his family's history. Holmes, for his part, often mused on the case, on how a man's curiosity could unearth not just treasure but ruin, on how some secrets were better left to slumber in the dark. And I, who had listened to the tale unfold, could not shake the image of Brunton's outstretched hand, reaching for a prize that had cost him everything.
