## st-0222 — The Country of the Blind
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
A man walks in high land. He finds a new place.

People live there. They do not see. They can not see.

The place has a name. It is the land of no eyes. No one from far knows it.

One day, a man comes. His name is Nuñez. He falls down a high place. He is in the low land now.

Nuñez thinks he can lead them. He says, 'In the land of no eyes,' 'the man with one eye can lead.'

Nuñez tries to tell them. He points at far things. He talks about colors.

The people do not laugh. They say his words make no sense. They do not need help.

The town is built well. Everything helps them move. They do not need light.

A young woman hears Nuñez. Her name is Medina-saroté. She is kind to him.

Nuñez likes her. He wants to stay. He wants to be with her. But the first people say no.

The first people say his eyes are bad. They say his eyes cause a bad feel. They want to take his eyes out.

Nuñez does not want this. He thinks about going away. He does not know the way out.

One night, Nuñez decides to leave. He goes up the high land. He uses his eyes to find the way.

It is hard to go up. He almost falls many times. He reaches the top.

He looks back at the town. He sees Medina-saroté far down. She can not see him.

Nuñez leaves the low land. He goes back to his world. People there can see.

He tells his story. Many do not believe him. He thinks about Medina-saroté.

He knows he can not go back. In the low land, eyes are not good.

### A2
High up, there was a place far from other towns. No one from outside knew about it.

People there could not see. They had lived that way for many years.

The place was called the Country of the Blind.

One day, a man named Nuñez came to the place. He fell down a high rock while he walked.

When he opened his eyes, he was in that place. He thought he could be the leader there.

He said, 'In a place where people cannot see,' 'a man who can see is the leader.'

Nuñez tried to tell the people about seeing. They did not understand what he said.

They thought he acted in a new way. He showed them things far away.

He told them about colors. The people only laughed at him.

The people lived well without seeing. They built their houses in a good way.

Everything helped them move around easily. They did not need light to live.

Nuñez saw they did not want his help. They did not like his new ideas.

A young woman named Medina-saroté liked Nuñez. She listened to the things he said.

Nuñez began to love her. He wanted to stay and marry her.

The leaders of the village said he must change. They said his eyes were a big problem.

His eyes made him act in a new way. They wanted to take his eyes out.

Nuñez did not want to lose his eyes. He thought about running away.

One night, he decided to leave. He walked up the high rocks.

The walk was very hard. He almost fell many times.

At the top, he looked back down. He saw Medina-saroté below him.

She could not see him. Nuñez left the Country of the Blind.

He went back to the world where people could see. He told his story to others.

Many did not believe what he said. He often thought about Medina-saroté.

He knew he could never go back. Seeing was not important there.

Nuñez remembered the people in the village. They lived a good life without seeing.

He thought about how they did not need light. They built everything in a way that helped them.

He remembered Medina-saroté’s kind face. She was the only one who listened to him.

Nuñez knew he had to leave. He could not let them take his eyes out.

The climb up the rocks was hard. He was happy when he reached the top.

He looked at the village one last time. Then he turned and walked away.

Nuñez wanted to tell others about the place. But most did not believe his story.

He often sat and thought about the village. He wondered if they still talked about him.

Medina-saroté might have forgotten him. But he would never forget her.

The world where people could see was different. But Nuñez knew he could not go back.

He had to live with his choice. Seeing was not everything, he learned.

### B1
High in the mountains of Ecuador, there was a place no one knew about.

For many years, people from outside did not find it.

The people there could not see.

They had lived without sight for a very long time.

The place was called the Country Where No One Sees.

One day, a man named Nuñez came to this place.

He had climbed a tall mountain but fell down the other side.

When he woke up, he was in the hidden place.

Nuñez thought he could lead these people because he could see.

He said to himself, 'Where no one sees, the man who sees is like a king.'

Nuñez tried to tell the people what sight was.

They did not understand him.

They thought he was strange.

He tried to show them how his eyes helped him.

He pointed at things far away and talked about their colors.

The people just laughed.

They said his words made no sense at all.

The people in this place lived well without sight.

They had built their town over many years.

Everything was made to help them move around easily.

They did not need light because they knew their home well.

Nuñez saw they did not want his help or his ideas.

A young woman named Medina was kind to him.

She listened to his stories.

Nuñez began to love her.

He wanted to stay and marry her.

But the leaders of the town said he must change first.

The leaders told Nuñez his eyes were a problem.

They said his eyes made him act in a strange way.

His eyes also caused him pain.

They said if he wanted to stay, they would take his eyes out.

Nuñez was very afraid and did not want to lose his sight.

He thought about running away but did not know the way out.

One night, he decided to leave the place.

He climbed up the tall rocks, using his eyes to find the path.

The climb was very hard.

He almost fell many times.

When he reached the top, he looked back at the town.

He saw Medina standing below, but she could not see him.

Nuñez left the Country Where No One Sees behind.

He went back to the world where people could see.

He told his story to others, but many did not believe him.

He often thought about Medina.

He thought about the life he almost had.

But he knew he could never go back.

In that place, sight had no value.

He wanted to keep his eyes more than anything.

Nuñez had learned something important.

Even if people do not see, they can live a good life.

But he also knew he needed to see to be happy.

He often looked at the mountains and remembered his time there.

He wondered if the people were still living well without sight.

He hoped Medina was happy.

Sometimes, he wished he could go back and try again.

But he knew it was not possible.

He had to live in the world where people could see.

Nuñez never forgot the place or the people.

He told his story many times.

Some people listened, but others just laughed.

He always thought about what he had learned.

Sight was a gift, but not everyone needed it.

He was glad he had his eyes, but he also respected those who did not.

He knew that both ways of life had value.

Nuñez often wondered if he had made the right choice.

He still dreamed about the hidden place sometimes.

He hoped the people there were doing well.

He missed Medina and the life he could have had.

But he knew he could not change what had happened.

He decided to keep telling his story to others.

Maybe one day, someone would understand.

Until then, he would live in the world of sight.

He would always remember the Country Where No One Sees.

### B2
High in the mountains of Ecuador, there was a place no one outside knew about. For many years, this place stayed hidden from the rest of the world.

The people who lived there could not see. They had been without sight for many generations. The place was called the Country of the Blind.

One day, a man named Nuñez came to this hidden place. He had climbed a tall mountain but fell down the other side. When he woke up, he found himself in the valley.

Nuñez thought he could be the leader of these people. He said to himself, 'Where everyone is blind, the man who can see is king.'

Nuñez tried to explain what it meant to see. He told them about things far away and their colors. The people just laughed and said his words made no sense.

The people in the valley lived well without sight. They had built their town carefully over many years. Everything was made to help them move around easily.

They did not need light because they knew their home so well. Nuñez saw that they did not want his help or his ideas. They thought his eyes were strange and caused problems.

A young woman named Medina took an interest in Nuñez. She was kind to him and listened to his stories about the outside world. Nuñez began to love her and wanted to stay in the valley.

He wanted to marry her, but the leaders of the town said he must change first. They told Nuñez that his eyes were a problem. His eyes made him act in ways they did not understand.

The leaders suggested that if he wanted to stay, they should take his eyes away. Nuñez was shocked and did not want to lose his sight. He thought about leaving but did not know how to get out.

One night, Nuñez decided to run away from the valley. He started to climb up the high rocks, using his eyes to find the way. The climb was very hard, and he nearly fell many times.

When he reached the top, he looked back at the town one last time. He saw Medina standing below, but she could not see him. Nuñez left the Country of the Blind and returned to the world where people could see.

He told his story to others, but many did not believe him. He often thought about Medina and the life he almost had. But he knew he could never go back to a place where sight was not valued.

Nuñez had learned that being different was not always a good thing. In the valley, his eyes were seen as a problem, not a gift. He realized that what he thought was a strength was not wanted there.

The people in the valley had their own way of living. They did not need sight to be happy or to do their work. Everything was arranged so they could live without it.

Nuñez remembered how the town was built. The paths were smooth, and the houses were easy to find. There were no steps or things that could cause them to fall.

He also remembered how the people worked together. They helped each other and knew where everything was. They did not need to see to know what to do.

Medina had been kind to him, but she could not understand his world. She had never seen the sky or the colors he described. To her, his words were just strange sounds.

Nuñez thought about what the leaders had said. They believed that if he lost his sight, he would be like them. He would no longer act strangely or cause them trouble.

But Nuñez knew he could not give up his eyes. His sight was part of who he was, and he did not want to live without it. He decided that leaving was the only choice he had.

The climb out of the valley was harder than he expected. He had to be very careful not to fall. His eyes helped him see the path, but the rocks were loose and slippery.

When he finally reached the top, he felt a mix of feelings. He was happy to be free but sad to leave Medina behind. He wondered if she would ever know what happened to him.

Back in the world where people could see, Nuñez told his story. Some people listened, but others thought he was making it up. He realized that not everyone would believe something so strange.

He often thought about the valley and the people there. He wondered if they ever thought about him or the outside world. But he knew he could not go back, no matter how much he wanted to.

Nuñez learned that being different was not always easy. In the valley, his eyes made him an outsider. He had to accept that some places were not for him.

He also learned that people could live well without things he thought were necessary. The people in the valley did not need sight to be happy. They had their own way of life, and it worked for them.

Even though he missed Medina, Nuñez knew he had made the right choice. He could not live in a place where his eyes were seen as a problem. He had to return to the world where people valued what he could see.

The story of Nuñez and the Country of the Blind became a lesson for him. He understood that not every place would accept him as he was. He had to find where he truly belonged.

### C1
The Andes rose like the spine of some ancient, slumbering beast, their peaks clawing at the belly of the sky. For centuries, a hidden valley nestled between these jagged ridges, untouched by the world beyond-a place where the wind carried no echoes of distant cities, and the rain fell in silence, as if the mountains themselves had conspired to keep it secret. Here, the people of the valley had lived for generations without sight, their eyes as blind as the stones beneath their feet, yet their hands knew every contour of their world with a precision that sighted folk could scarcely imagine. They called their home the Country of the Blind, not in sorrow, but in quiet pride, for they had shaped their existence without the need for light, and in doing so, had woven a life that was whole in ways the seeing could never understand.

Nunez had not meant to find them. A climber by trade and a dreamer by nature, he had set out to conquer the last uncharted face of the great peak, driven by the same restless curiosity that had always set him apart from those content with the familiar. But the mountain had other plans-one moment he was gripping the rock, the next he was tumbling, the world a blur of sky and stone, until he crashed into the soft earth of the valley below, his body broken but his mind still clinging to consciousness. When he awoke, the air was thick with the scent of damp earth and something sweeter, like crushed herbs, and the first thing he saw was the sky, impossibly vast and blue, stretching above him like a promise.

He sat up slowly, his ribs protesting, and took in the valley for the first time. The town was a marvel of order, its paths smooth and unbroken, its buildings low and rounded, their walls marked with raised patterns that he later learned were a kind of tactile language, a map for fingers instead of eyes. People moved through the streets with an ease that bordered on grace, their hands brushing the walls, their steps sure and unhurried, as if the very ground beneath them whispered its secrets. Nunez watched, his breath catching in his throat, as a group of children played a game of tag, their laughter ringing out in the still air, their blindness no impediment to their joy.

It was then that the thought took root in him, insidious and intoxicating: *In the country of the blind, the one-eyed man is king.* He had read the proverb in some dusty book long ago, and now it seemed to him not just a clever saying, but a truth as solid as the mountains themselves. With his sight, he could navigate the world in ways these people could not even imagine-he could see the dangers before they stumbled into them, could describe the colors of the sky, the shapes of the clouds, the way the stars burned like distant fires. Surely, they would welcome him, would look to him for guidance, would revere him as something more than just another pair of hands in the dark.

He was wrong. When he tried to explain sight to them, his words fell flat, like stones dropped into a well with no bottom. They listened, their faces tilted toward him in polite confusion, but their expressions remained blank, as if he were speaking in a language of sounds they could not parse. A man with a long, silver-streaked beard finally interrupted him, his voice gentle but firm: "You speak of things that do not exist, stranger. The world is what we touch, what we hear, what we know. Your *seeing* is a dream, and dreams are for children." Nunez opened his mouth to argue, but the words died on his lips, for how could he explain the color of a sunset to those who had never seen the sun?

Yet not all of them dismissed him so easily. Medina, a woman with hands as quick and sure as any weaver's, took an interest in him, her curiosity piqued by his strange manner of moving, the way his eyes darted about as if searching for something just out of reach. She brought him food-flatbreads warm from the oven, stews thick with roots and herbs-and listened as he spoke of the world beyond the valley, of cities that stretched to the horizon, of ships that sailed across endless waters. Her questions were simple, but her attention was rapt, and in her presence, Nunez felt something he had not known he was missing: the quiet comfort of being truly heard.

Over time, his feelings for her deepened, not into the fiery passion of his youthful dreams, but into something steadier, something that rooted itself in his chest like the slow growth of a tree. He began to imagine a life in the valley, a life where he could teach them, not as a king, but as a partner, where his sight might one day be seen not as a flaw, but as a bridge between their world and the one beyond. But the elders of the valley had other plans. They summoned him before them, their faces unreadable in the dim light of the meeting hall, and told him plainly that if he wished to stay, his eyes must go.

The words struck him like a physical blow. He had known, of course, that his eyes unsettled them-had seen the way they flinched when he turned his gaze upon them, the way their hands twitched as if to shield themselves from something invisible. But to suggest that he carve out a part of himself, to ask him to blind himself as casually as one might trim a fingernail-that was a cruelty he had not anticipated. He stammered out a refusal, his voice raw with shock, and the elders exchanged glances, their expressions unchanging, as if his distress were nothing more than the whining of a child denied a toy.

That night, he lay awake, the weight of his decision pressing down on him like the mountain itself. He thought of Medina, of the way her fingers had traced the lines of his palm as if memorizing him, of the softness in her voice when she spoke his name. But he thought, too, of the valley's smooth paths, of the way the people moved in perfect harmony with their world, of the quiet certainty with which they lived, unburdened by the chaos of sight. To stay would be to surrender a part of himself, to become something less than he was, and yet to leave would be to lose her, perhaps forever.

At last, he made his choice. Before the first light of dawn touched the peaks, he slipped from the house where he had been given shelter, his pack light, his heart heavy. The climb out of the valley was worse than he remembered-loose rocks skittering beneath his boots, the wind howling like a living thing, the sheer drop yawning beside him at every turn. But his eyes, those same eyes the valley had scorned, guided him, picking out the safest path, the sturdiest handholds, the way forward when all else was darkness.

When he reached the summit, he turned one last time, his breath ragged, his hands trembling. Far below, the valley lay like a bowl of shadow, its people stirring to wakefulness, unaware that he was gone. He thought he saw a figure standing at the edge of the town, a woman with her face turned toward the sky, but the distance was too great, and he could not be sure it was her. Then the wind rose, biting and cold, and he turned away, stepping back into the world of the seeing, where his story would be met with disbelief, where his eyes would be valued, but where he would always carry the weight of what he had almost become.

Years later, when travelers asked him why he had left, he would speak of the valley with a strange mixture of longing and relief, his voice low, as if the memory itself were something fragile. He would tell them of the people who had no need for sight, who had built a life so complete that his own eyes had been not a gift, but an intrusion. And sometimes, in the quiet hours before dawn, when the world was still and the stars burned brightest, he would wonder if Medina ever thought of him, if she ever stood at the edge of the valley and looked up at the sky, searching for something she could not name.

---
